# Report

Generated by `nndc report`. Checks compare each program with the original net (never the target); `task in / out` grades against the true task inside and outside the training range (accuracy, or max abs error for regression). Input names are shown with their real names here; the refactorer only saw `x0, x1, …`.

## How to read the table

Each model has one row per program version: `dump` is the raw network written as code, `agent_N` is
a Claude Code refactoring of it.

- **run**: `task/kind`; `hand` = handcrafted net, `seedN` = trained with that seed.
- **training**: how well the *network* learned the real task (accuracy for classifiers, R² for
  regression). "NOT learned" marks a training failure (kept, never replaced).
- **version**: which program the row is about.
- **check**: the program compared with the *network* (never the target).
  **PASS exact** = same answers everywhere, up to float rounding; **PASS snap** = differs only where
  allowed (below); **FAIL** = differs where it must not; **ERROR** = invalid program.
- **label**: how the check was done. **exhaustive** = every possible input tried (a proof for that
  domain); **empirical** = about 280k sampled inputs, concentrated near decision boundaries (strong
  testing, not proof).
- **max err**: regression only; the largest |program − network|. Snap allows up to 2% of the
  network's output range.
- **disagree outside / inside (zone)**: classifiers only. *Outside* = disagreements where the network
  was confident (must be 0 to pass); *inside* = disagreements in the network's "unsure zone" near its
  decision line (allowed). The % is the share of the input space inside that zone. The `dump` rows
  show large *inside* counts that are only float32-vs-float64 rounding ties exactly on the decision
  line, not real disagreements.
- **task in / out**: the program graded against the *true* answer, report only. *in* = inside the
  training range, *out* = up to 50% beyond it (generalization). Accuracy for classifiers (1 = perfect),
  max abs error for regression (0 = perfect). Blank *out* = discrete inputs, nothing outside.
- **recovered**: yes = the program is the true algorithm (perfect inside and outside the range).
- **nodes (% of dump)**: program size in syntax-tree nodes, and as a share of the dump.
- **audit**: Claude runs only. **ok** = the transcript shows no file access outside the blind folder;
  **OUTSIDE PATHS** = it touched outside files (such runs are excluded from headline numbers);
  **no transcript** = could not be audited.
- **Claude**: time taken and input+output tokens (input includes cached context).


## Where the refactored program beats the network

Runs where a refactored program is closer to the *true task* than the network it was decompiled from (better inside or outside the training range, and not worse on either). The refactorer never saw the target; this is the "snapping recovers the intended algorithm" effect.

## Kink merge (ast4)

The kink-merge route turns each sigmoid into straight pieces, merges the kinks that lie on one line, keeps the strongest lines and refits them to the net (`ast4`). Held-out runs are listed in `heldout.json` (frozen at `5ed6ee8`); the other runs are development runs. Only runs where the route applies (one sigmoid or tanh layer, not argmax) are listed. *kinks → lines → nearly cancelled → kept* are the counts for the kink-merge candidate's merge tolerance; where another route won, its lines are marked *candidate only* and are not in `ast4.py`.

- **held-out**: 5 of 5 pass, kink-merge won 3, recovered the formula in 1; reproduces Claude's best valid attempt in 0 of 0
- **development**: 15 of 15 pass, kink-merge won 10, recovered the formula in 3; reproduces Claude's best valid attempt in 7 of 14

| run | split | route | ast4 vs net | recovered | nodes | kink-merge: kinks → lines → nearly cancelled → kept | kink-merge lines |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| absdiff/seed0 | development | kink-merge | PASS snap | no | 135 | 128 → 48 → 2 → 6 | 0.654 · max(0.99 * a - b + 0.4, 0); 0.714 · max(0.99 * a - b - 0.4, 0); 0.54 · max(0.99 * a - b + 1.25, 0); 0.44 · max(0.99 * a - b - 1.25, 0); -0.675 · max(0.98 * a - b + 3.8, 0); -0.606 · max(a - b - 4, 0) |

## Reproduction (ast4)

Does the AST tool `ast4` (frozen at `5ed6ee8`) reproduce Claude's best valid attempt? Reproduces = passes the check, same recovered status as Claude, and at most max(1.5x, +5) of Claude's nodes. Held-out runs are listed in `heldout.json` and were built after the freeze.

- **held-out**: 5 of 5 pass; recovered the formula in 1; reproduces Claude in 0 of 0 with a valid attempt
- **development**: 18 of 18 pass; recovered the formula in 3; reproduces Claude in 7 of 15 with a valid attempt

## Real datasets

Dataset runs (Iris) have no known formula: their task columns below compare with the label of the nearest table row, and `recovered` is n/a. They are left out of the step-3 reproduction and the beats-the-network list above. Their results and held-out runs are in [runs_tanh/iris/REPORT.md](runs_tanh/iris/REPORT.md).

## absdiff/seed0: dump vs agent_1

```python
# dumped from a neural network: 2 inputs -> 8 -> 1 outputs
# inputs: a: float [-3, 3], b: float [-3, 3]
# output: regression: the returned value
def net(a, b):
    h1_0 = tanh(0.02685782 * a + 0.029734058 * b + 1.4272918)  # layer 1 neuron 0
    h1_1 = tanh(-0.03329181 * a - 0.029165335 * b + 1.3440735)  # layer 1 neuron 1
    h1_2 = tanh(-0.38088593 * a + 0.38900813 * b - 0.7866674)  # layer 1 neuron 2
    h1_3 = tanh(0.02962898 * a + 0.025462113 * b - 1.1097628)  # layer 1 neuron 3
    h1_4 = tanh(-0.3784108 * a + 0.3762217 * b + 0.7849715)  # layer 1 neuron 4
    h1_5 = tanh(-0.085820794 * a - 0.080495246 * b + 1.2818668)  # layer 1 neuron 5
    h1_6 = tanh(-0.05922657 * a - 0.06235523 * b - 1.179106)  # layer 1 neuron 6
    h1_7 = tanh(0.022196637 * a + 0.026969722 * b + 1.1889496)  # layer 1 neuron 7
    return 1.0768071 * h1_0 + 0.92706853 * h1_1 + 3.799525 * h1_2 - 1.2873737 * h1_3 - 3.871807 * h1_4 + 0.5983937 * h1_5 - 1.1552155 * h1_6 + 1.0367786 * h1_7 + 0.35808393
```

```python
def net(a, b):
    # The output is a smooth "distance from the diagonal" curve: low (~0.4) when a == b,
    # rising to ~5 as |b - a| grows. Two mirrored tanh steps form an even dip in diff.
    diff = b - a
    total = a + b
    dip = tanh(0.38 * diff + 0.78) - tanh(0.38 * diff - 0.78)
    return 5.5 - 3.9 * dip + 0.015 * total
```

AST tool (no LLM, `ast4`):

```python
# produced by nndc ast (route: kink-merge)
def net(a, b):
    return -0.51 * a + 0.5351348927718301 * b + 2.0648765390227126 + 0.6542198254136445 * relu(0.99 * a - b + 0.4) + 0.7135967710863883 * relu(0.99 * a - b - 0.4) + 0.54 * relu(0.99 * a - b + 1.25) + 0.44 * relu(0.99 * a - b - 1.25) - 0.6752108616106435 * relu(0.98 * a - b + 3.8) - 0.6062529974718132 * relu(a - b - 4)
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network does
Output is a regression value that depends almost only on `diff = x1 - x0`: about 0.45 on the
diagonal x0 == x1, rising smoothly and symmetrically to about 5.1 at |diff| = 6. Along the diagonal
(`total = x0 + x1`) it moves only between 0.27 and 0.48.

#### What I changed
- Units 2 and 4 have weights about (-0.38, +0.38) on (x0, x1), biases about -0.79 and +0.78, and output
  weights about +3.8 and -3.87. Together they are one even "dip" in `diff`:
  `tanh(k*diff + b) - tanh(k*diff - b)`. I merged them into one shared slope 0.38, offset 0.78 and
  amplitude 3.9.
- The other six units have very small weights (|w| < 0.09) along x0 + x1. Over [-6, 6] they are close
  to linear, so they become a constant plus a small slope: `5.5 + 0.015 * total`. The constant 5.5
  also takes the saturated parts of the dip pair. Without the slope term the error is 0.16, above the
  0.098 tolerance. With it the error is 0.055.
- Snapped values: 0.38, 0.78, 3.9, 5.5, 0.015. I chose them by a small grid search over nearby round
  values, after a least-squares fit of the form `c - A*dip(k, b) + cs*total` on a dense grid.
- Result: `PASS (snap)`, max |program - net| = 0.055 (tolerance 0.098). Eight tanh units became two.

#### Heuristics
- Look at the first-layer weight vectors. Units whose weights point the same way, here (-1, +1),
  depend only on that projection. Rotate the inputs into `diff = x1 - x0` and `total = x0 + x1`
  before you analyse anything.
- Two tanh units with the same slope, opposite biases (+b, -b) and opposite output weights of about
  the same size form an even bump or dip: `A*(tanh(k*u + b) - tanh(k*u - b))`. Merge them with a shared
  k, b and A.
- Units whose input weights are tiny compared with the input range (|w|*range < ~0.5) work as a
  constant plus a linear term. Fold them into one bias and a small linear term. Check whether the
  linear term is needed against the tolerance.
- Print 1-D profiles along the main projections (here diff at total = 0, and total at diff = 0). They
  show the function's shape and how big the leftover variation is, before any fitting.
- Fit a few small parametric forms with least squares, then grid-search rounded values near the fit
  and keep the simplest one that stays well inside the snap tolerance.

</details>

## absdiff/seed1: dump vs agent_1

```python
# dumped from a neural network: 2 inputs -> 8 -> 1 outputs
# inputs: a: float [-3, 3], b: float [-3, 3]
# output: regression: the returned value
def net(a, b):
    h1_0 = tanh(0.4181785 * a - 0.41319197 * b - 1.50573)  # layer 1 neuron 0
    h1_1 = tanh(-0.011226631 * a + 0.00069445255 * b - 1.9144812)  # layer 1 neuron 1
    h1_2 = tanh(-0.027993757 * a - 0.041580807 * b + 1.6560346)  # layer 1 neuron 2
    h1_3 = tanh(0.018304301 * a + 0.003993939 * b + 1.302705)  # layer 1 neuron 3
    h1_4 = tanh(-0.013836722 * a + 0.0019883232 * b - 1.0235078)  # layer 1 neuron 4
    h1_5 = tanh(1.0536809 * a - 1.0545524 * b - 0.6439078)  # layer 1 neuron 5
    h1_6 = tanh(0.414329 * a - 0.41511044 * b + 1.5254393)  # layer 1 neuron 6
    h1_7 = tanh(1.0242267 * a - 1.0173615 * b + 0.62451506)  # layer 1 neuron 7
    return 2.6326067 * h1_0 - 1.8277428 * h1_1 + 1.5261128 * h1_2 + 1.511807 * h1_3 - 1.2463346 * h1_4 + 1.1531957 * h1_5 - 2.6666424 * h1_6 - 1.1903093 * h1_7 + 0.8693279
```

```python
def net(a, b):
    # The network depends only on the difference of its inputs; it is a smooth |a - b|.
    d = a - b
    # two even "dips" made of a pair of mirrored tanh steps each:
    #   tanh(s*d - b) - tanh(s*d + b) is about -2 near d = 0 and goes to 0 as |d| grows
    wide = tanh(5 * d / 12 - 1.5) - tanh(5 * d / 12 + 1.5)
    narrow = tanh(d - 0.6) - tanh(d + 0.6)
    # the four near-constant hidden units of the dump fold into the constant 6.2
    return 6.2 + 2.6 * wide + 1.2 * narrow
```

AST tool (no LLM, `ast4`):

```python
# produced by nndc ast (route: kink-merge)
def net(a, b):
    return -0.65 * a + 0.65 * b + 1.75 + 0.95 * relu(a - b - 0.25) + 1.05 * relu(a - b + 0.25) - 0.35 * relu(a - b + 5) - 0.32 * relu(a - b - 5)
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network computes

A smooth version of `|x0 - x1|`. The output is about 0.18 at x0 = x1, about |d| for 1 <= |d| <= 5,
and it flattens to about 5.64 at the corners (|d| = 6), where training data is rare.

#### What I changed

- **Near-constant units (h1_1..h1_4).** Their input weights are at most 0.04 and their biases are
  1.0 to 1.9, so over [-3, 3]^2 each stays near its value at the origin. The output weights times
  `tanh(bias)` plus the output bias sum to about 6.30. All four units fold into one constant.
- **Difference units (h1_0, h1_5, h1_6, h1_7).** Each has weights (+w, -w) on (x0, x1), so the net
  depends only on d = x0 - x1. They come in mirrored pairs with opposite output weights:
  - h1_0 / h1_6: slope about 0.415, biases -1.506 / +1.525, weights +2.63 / -2.67;
  - h1_5 / h1_7: slope about 1.04, biases -0.644 / +0.625, weights +1.15 / -1.19.

  Each pair is `A * (tanh(s*d - b) - tanh(s*d + b))`. That is an even dip in d: about -2A*tanh(b)
  at d = 0, going to 0 as |d| grows. Two dips subtracted from a constant give the V shape of |d|.
- **Snapping.** Slopes go to 5/12 and 1, offsets to 1.5 and 0.6, and the constants are refitted by
  least squares and rounded (6.2, 2.6, 1.2). I checked several snap sets. 5/12 for the wide pair
  mattered: 0.4 or 0.45 cost much more accuracy.
- **Simpler forms fail.** A single tanh pair (max error 0.36), and `c + a*d*tanh(k*d) + q*d^2`
  (0.38), are both far beyond the 0.11 tolerance.

Result: `PASS (snap)`, max |program - net| = 0.064 against a tolerance of 0.110, worst at the
corners x0 = -x1 = ±3. An exact pass would need the original weights.

#### Heuristics

- Check whether the hidden units' input weights come in (+w, -w) patterns or other shared
  directions. If every unit that matters has the same direction, the net is a 1-D function of one
  projection (here x0 - x1). Rewrite the program in that variable and probe the net along it.
- Units whose input weights are tiny compared with their bias (|w| * input range << |bias|) are
  constants over the domain. Fold `w_out * act(bias)` into the output bias. Their combined
  variation can still reach a few hundredths, so check the total against the tolerance.
- Two tanh (or sigmoid) units with the same slope, opposite biases (±b) and opposite output weights
  form an even bump or dip: `A * (tanh(s*d - b) - tanh(s*d + b))`. Merge them, averaging s, b and |A|.
- After choosing structure and snapping the nonlinear parameters (slopes, offsets), refit the linear
  parameters (output weights, constant) with least squares on random inputs. Then round them.
  This recovers most of the error the snapping introduced.
- The largest errors tend to be at the domain corners, where the net extrapolates. Compare
  candidates by max error over a sample that includes the corners, not by RMS.

</details>

```python
# output: regression: the returned value
def net(a, b):
    h1_0 = tanh(-0.015404745 * a - 0.023808718 * b + 1.2694436)  # layer 1 neuron 0
    h1_1 = tanh(0.02144565 * a + 0.030097997 * b - 1.3365957)  # layer 1 neuron 1
    h1_2 = tanh(0.014730033 * a + 0.023361968 * b - 1.1983639)  # layer 1 neuron 2
    h1_3 = tanh(0.38430732 * a - 0.3782352 * b + 0.79996055)  # layer 1 neuron 3
    h1_4 = tanh(-0.09399711 * a - 0.091604464 * b - 1.4694228)  # layer 1 neuron 4
    h1_5 = tanh(-0.031926114 * a - 0.02375233 * b - 0.80307746)  # layer 1 neuron 5
    h1_6 = tanh(0.037475582 * a + 0.047150232 * b - 1.1160344)  # layer 1 neuron 6
    h1_7 = tanh(-0.38143086 * a + 0.38741243 * b + 0.80412215)  # layer 1 neuron 7
    return 0.90038735 * h1_0 - 0.75941896 * h1_1 - 0.80066246 * h1_2 - 3.750011 * h1_3 - 0.9348898 * h1_4 - 0.9052237 * h1_5 - 1.5764955 * h1_6 - 3.70987 * h1_7 + 0.6397758
```

```python
def net(a, b):
    d = a - b
    s = a + b
    # symmetric bump in the difference: a smoothed |a - b|
    valley = tanh(0.38 * d + 0.8) + tanh(0.8 - 0.38 * d)
    # weak, slightly curved trend along the sum (from 6 near-linear units)
    trend = -s / 50 - s * s / 400
    return 5.475 - 3.75 * valley + trend
```

AST tool (no LLM, `ast4`):

```python
# produced by nndc ast (route: kink-merge)
def net(a, b):
    return -0.52 * a + 0.48 * b + 2.163039572671957 + 0.6 * relu(a - b - 0.5) + 0.7 * relu(a - b + 0.25) + 0.4 * relu(a - b - 1.25) + 0.58 * relu(a - b + 1.25) - 0.625 * relu(a - b - 4) - 0.65 * relu(a - b + 4)
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network does
2 inputs in [-3, 3], 8 tanh units, regression output (range about 0.29 .. 5.12).

- Units 3 and 7 are a mirrored pair: `tanh(0.384*(x0-x1) + 0.80)` and `tanh(-0.384*(x0-x1) + 0.80)`,
  both with output weight about -3.73. Their sum is an even function of `d = x0 - x1`, so together
  they form a smooth "valley" in d: the output is a smoothed `|x0 - x1|` (about 0.5 at d = 0,
  about 5.1 at |d| = 6, slope about 1.2 in the middle).
- The other six units (0, 1, 2, 4, 5, 6) have tiny input weights (|w| < 0.1) along roughly the
  `x0 + x1` direction, so on the box they work in the near-linear part of tanh. Together they add
  only a constant plus a weak trend along `s = x0 + x1` (the diagonal drifts from 0.50 to 0.29).

#### Final program (`agent.py`)
```
d = x0 - x1;  s = x0 + x1
valley = tanh(0.38*d + 0.8) + tanh(0.8 - 0.38*d)
return 5.475 - 3.75*valley - s/50 - s*s/400
```
PASS (snap), max |program - net| = 0.028 against a tolerance of 0.097.

#### How I got there
1. Spotted the mirrored pair (3, 7) by comparing weight vectors: equal magnitudes, opposite signs,
   same bias. Rewrote both in terms of `d`, with averaged slope, bias and output weight.
2. Least-squares fitted the residual (net minus the pair) with constant + linear terms: the
   residual depends almost only on `s`. A linear term in `s` alone left 0.086 max error, which is too
   close to the tolerance. Adding `s^2` brought it down to 0.011.
3. Nonlinear fit of the whole model `c - w*valley(a, b) + k*s + q*s^2`, then snapped to
   a = 0.38, b = 0.8, w = 3.75, k = -1/50, q = -1/400. Then re-centred the constant (the midpoint
   of the min/max error) to c = 5.475, which halved the max error (0.051 -> 0.028).
   Snapping `a` to 3/8 instead of 0.38 nearly doubled the error, so `a` stays at 0.38 (the steep
   pair is the sensitive part, with output weight ~3.75).

An exact pass would need all eight units (no two are exact duplicates), so I aimed for snap.

#### Heuristics
- Look for mirrored unit pairs (weights w and -w, same bias, same output weight). Their sum is an
  even function of `w.x`: a bump or valley, i.e. a smoothed `|w.x|`. Rewrite them over a shared
  named variable such as `d = x0 - x1`.
- Units whose input weights are tiny relative to the input range (|w| * range << 1) sit in the
  near-linear part of tanh/sigmoid. Fit their combined contribution with a low-order polynomial
  (constant, linear, then quadratic if needed) in the direction they share, instead of keeping them.
- Fit the residual after removing the dominant structure. It tells you which direction the leftover
  units care about (here `x0 + x1`), and how many polynomial terms you need.
- After snapping the coefficients, re-fit only the additive constant to the midpoint of the error
  range (minimax shift). It is free and often halves the max error.
- Snap the parameters of high-gain units (large output weight times steep slope) less aggressively.
  Their error is amplified, so keep two significant digits there and snap the low-gain ones harder.
- Keep a margin: target a max error well below the snap tolerance (here under 1/3 of it), because
  the checker also uses boundary and corner points that a random sample may miss.

</details>

## iris/seed0: dump vs agent_1

```python
# dumped from a neural network: 4 inputs -> 8 -> 3 outputs
# inputs: sepal_length: float [4.3, 7.9], sepal_width: float [2, 4.4], petal_length: float [1, 6.9], petal_width: float [0.1, 2.5]
# output: argmax over 3 outputs: the returned class index
def net(sepal_length, sepal_width, petal_length, petal_width):
    h1_0 = tanh(0.20915557 * sepal_length + 0.97714597 * sepal_width - 1.2296648 * petal_length - 1.4137415 * petal_width + 0.2106613)  # layer 1 neuron 0
    h1_1 = tanh(-0.3111128 * sepal_length - 0.6618526 * sepal_width + 0.83770585 * petal_length + 1.358329 * petal_width - 2.455376)  # layer 1 neuron 1
    h1_2 = tanh(0.22572677 * sepal_length + 0.9982923 * sepal_width - 0.99315006 * petal_length - 1.0585442 * petal_width + 1.7542416)  # layer 1 neuron 2
    h1_3 = tanh(-0.22424622 * sepal_length - 0.7855879 * sepal_width + 0.3534074 * petal_length - 0.37646902 * petal_width + 2.0515585)  # layer 1 neuron 3
    h1_4 = tanh(0.3309654 * sepal_length + 0.42904288 * sepal_width - 0.2000414 * petal_length - 0.07587654 * petal_width - 0.16844493)  # layer 1 neuron 4
    h1_5 = tanh(-0.43036488 * sepal_length - 0.60099655 * sepal_width + 0.9144643 * petal_length + 1.466971 * petal_width - 2.2093003)  # layer 1 neuron 5
    h1_6 = tanh(-0.46066856 * sepal_length - 0.5084488 * sepal_width + 1.5028943 * petal_length + 0.5278546 * petal_width - 0.2156542)  # layer 1 neuron 6
    h1_7 = tanh(0.16954611 * sepal_length + 0.8715727 * sepal_width - 1.458632 * petal_length - 1.0187017 * petal_width + 1.0642788)  # layer 1 neuron 7
    o0 = 1.1602148 * h1_0 - 1.7312089 * h1_1 + 0.9912253 * h1_2 - 0.7883227 * h1_3 - 0.07372347 * h1_4 - 1.5489537 * h1_5 - 1.1835763 * h1_6 + 2.2724361 * h1_7 + 0.22027731  # output 0
    o1 = -1.9985185 * h1_0 - 2.8546815 * h1_1 + 1.7957683 * h1_2 + 1.7093502 * h1_3 + 0.0313787 * h1_4 - 1.9895995 * h1_5 + 0.868556 * h1_6 - 2.6646402 * h1_7 + 0.11622863  # output 1
    o2 = -0.34734452 * h1_0 + 2.375945 * h1_1 - 2.1170235 * h1_2 - 1.4147782 * h1_3 - 0.050398316 * h1_4 + 2.34728 * h1_5 - 0.024120592 * h1_6 - 0.33277905 * h1_7 - 0.10284828  # output 2
    return argmax(o0, o1, o2)
```

```python
# Three classes cut out by four planes; every plane is solved for petal_length.
# Class 0: petal_length is small (below both "low" planes).
# Class 2: petal_length is large (above both "high" planes).
# Class 1: everything in between.
def net(sepal_length, sepal_width, petal_length, petal_width):
    low_a = petal_length + 0.7 * petal_width - 0.18 * sepal_length - 2 / 3 * sepal_width
    low_b = petal_length + 0.75 * petal_width - sepal_length / 6 - 0.6 * sepal_width
    if low_a < 0.1 and low_b < 0.46:
        return 0
    high_a = petal_length + 1.8 * petal_width - 0.36 * sepal_length - 0.62 * sepal_width
    high_b = petal_length + 1.5 * petal_width - sepal_length / 3 - 0.8 * sepal_width
    if high_a > 3.87 and high_b > 2.96:
        return 2
    return 1
```

AST tool (no LLM, `ast4`):

```python
    h1_2 = sigmoid(0.45145354 * sepal_length + 1.9965847 * sepal_width - 1.9863001 * petal_length - 2.1170883 * petal_width + 3.5084832)
    h1_3 = sigmoid(-0.44849244 * sepal_length - 1.5711758 * sepal_width + 0.7068148 * petal_length - 0.75293803 * petal_width + 4.103117)
    h1_4 = sigmoid(0.6619308 * sepal_length + 0.85808575 * sepal_width - 0.4000828 * petal_length - 0.15175308 * petal_width - 0.33688986)
    h1_5 = sigmoid(-0.86072975 * sepal_length - 1.2019931 * sepal_width + 1.8289286 * petal_length + 2.933942 * petal_width - 4.4186006)
    h1_6 = sigmoid(-0.9213371 * sepal_length - 1.0168976 * sepal_width + 3.0057886 * petal_length + 1.0557092 * petal_width - 0.4313084)
    h1_7 = sigmoid(0.33909222 * sepal_length + 1.7431453 * sepal_width - 2.917264 * petal_length - 2.0374033 * petal_width + 2.1285577)
    o0 = 2.3204296 * h1_0 - 3.4624178 * h1_1 + 1.9824506 * h1_2 - 1.5766454 * h1_3 - 0.14744695 * h1_4 - 3.0979073 * h1_5 - 2.3671527 * h1_6 + 4.5448723 * h1_7 + 1.1221862
    o1 = -3.997037 * h1_0 - 5.709363 * h1_1 + 3.5915365 * h1_2 + 3.4187005 * h1_3 + 0.0627574 * h1_4 - 3.979199 * h1_5 + 1.737112 * h1_6 - 5.3292804 * h1_7 + 5.218615
    o2 = -0.69468904 * h1_0 + 4.75189 * h1_1 - 4.234047 * h1_2 - 2.8295565 * h1_3 - 0.10079663 * h1_4 + 4.69456 * h1_5 - 0.048241183 * h1_6 - 0.6655581 * h1_7 - 0.5396292
    return argmax(o0, o1, o2)
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network does

4 inputs -> 8 tanh units -> 3 class scores, argmax. Looking at the decision regions (not at the
individual units), I found:

- Class 0 and class 2 never touch. Bisecting random segments onto class changes gave only 0|1 and
  1|2 boundary points, no 0|2 points. So the net is an ordered 3-way split: 0 < 1 < 2.
- Both boundaries are nearly flat planes. Solved for x2 (the input with the largest weight),
  they are:
  - 0|1: x2 ~ 0.17 x0 + 0.63 x1 - 0.72 x3 + 0.29;
  - 1|2: x2 ~ 0.35 x0 + 0.69 x1 - 1.70 x3 + 3.63.

  Both bend slightly near the edges of the box (in the x3 and x1 directions), where the tanh units
  saturate. A single plane per boundary gave 460 confident errors. A max-margin LP showed that no
  single plane separates the confident points.
- Each class region on the "outside" is convex. The region below the 0|1 boundary and the region
  above the 1|2 boundary are each the intersection of two half-spaces. Two planes per boundary
  separate the confident points with no errors.

#### The program

```
low_a  = x2 + 0.7*x3  - 0.18*x0 - 2/3*x1     class 0 if low_a < 0.1 and low_b < 0.46
low_b  = x2 + 0.75*x3 - x0/6    - 0.6*x1
high_a = x2 + 1.8*x3  - 0.36*x0 - 0.62*x1    class 2 if high_a > 3.87 and high_b > 2.96
high_b = x2 + 1.5*x3  - x0/3    - 0.8*x1     otherwise class 1
```

All 8 tanh units and the 3 score lines are gone. What is left is 4 linear forms with snapped
coefficients and 4 thresholds. Check result: `PASS (snap)`, with 0 disagreements where the net is
confident; every disagreement is in the unsure zone. On 1M fresh uniform points plus 300k more,
there are also 0 confident disagreements. The original threshold 3.88 had one, and lowering it to
3.87 fixed that.

#### How I got there

1. I sampled the box and labelled each point with the net's class and margin, using "confident" =
   margin >= the checker's unsure threshold (0.5623).
2. I bisected random segments onto class changes. Fitting a plane (SVD) to each pair's boundary
   points showed which pairs share a boundary and how flat each one is (residuals).
3. I ran a max-margin LP (scipy linprog) per boundary, with the x2 coefficient fixed at 1. It was
   infeasible for one plane. I then fitted two planes by alternating: the class that must satisfy
   both planes is in both LPs, and each point of the other class is assigned to the plane that
   cuts it off. This converged to 0 errors in 2 iterations.
4. Snapping: I did a grid search over "nice" slope values (tenths, 0.05 steps, thirds, sixths)
   for both planes together. I scored each pair of planes by its slack, meaning how far the two
   thresholds can move together with every confident point still correct. I then chose
   thresholds inside the feasible window. The windows are narrow (about 0.02 in x2 units), so the
   thresholds need two decimals.
5. I validated on a large fresh sample, not just the checker's set, and moved one threshold to
   the safe side.

#### Heuristics

- For argmax nets, first find which class pairs actually share a boundary: bisect random segments
  onto class changes and tabulate the pairs. If some pair never touches (here 0|2), the classes
  are ordered, and the program becomes a decision list: test the extreme classes, and the middle
  class is the default.
- Fit a plane to each pair's boundary points (smallest singular vector) and write it solved for
  the input with the largest weight. Residuals binned by the other inputs show where the boundary
  bends. For tanh/sigmoid nets, the bends are at the box edges, where units saturate.
- If one plane is not enough, check which side is convex. If the boundary bends away from a
  class, that class's region is an intersection of half-spaces: `a < t1 and b < t2`. Two planes,
  fitted by alternating LPs, are usually enough for a small smooth net.
- Use the checker's unsure-zone threshold to define "confident" points, and require 0 errors on
  those only. That leaves a real gap, so an LP with a positive margin is feasible.
- Snap all the planes of one boundary together, and score each candidate by its joint slack (how
  far all its thresholds can move while it stays correct), not by its error count alone. Then
  pick round thresholds inside the window.
- Validate on a fresh, larger random sample (a different seed) as well as the checker's input
  set. Thresholds at the edge of the window fitted on one sample can fail on corner points of
  another sample. Move them toward the middle of the window.
- The tanh units themselves did not need to be understood one by one. Working on the decision
  geometry was far shorter than pruning or merging units.

</details>

```python
    h1_2 = tanh(-0.16363297 * sepal_length - 0.41357756 * sepal_width + 0.4511713 * petal_length + 1.2094487 * petal_width - 2.3986614)  # layer 1 neuron 2

    h1_4 = tanh(-0.2653208 * sepal_length - 0.8372558 * sepal_width + 1.2443165 * petal_length + 1.229876 * petal_width - 0.20522179)  # layer 1 neuron 4
    h1_5 = tanh(0.29679593 * sepal_length + 1.2638277 * sepal_width - 1.6596638 * petal_length - 2.235532 * petal_width + 0.9476771)  # layer 1 neuron 5
    h1_6 = tanh(-0.32043296 * sepal_length - 0.5822129 * sepal_width + 1.1029072 * petal_length + 1.141442 * petal_width - 0.21342961)  # layer 1 neuron 6
    h1_7 = tanh(-0.7145517 * sepal_length + 0.014678462 * sepal_width + 0.98123676 * petal_length + 1.3715549 * petal_width - 1.9627503)  # layer 1 neuron 7

    o1 = 1.3790774 * h1_0 + 0.58515847 * h1_1 - 3.2722478 * h1_2 - 2.1655924 * h1_3 + 2.2335148 * h1_4 - 0.96286 * h1_5 + 2.305024 * h1_6 - 1.610517 * h1_7 - 0.10030578  # output 1
    o2 = -1.9825077 * h1_0 - 0.5980745 * h1_1 + 2.9436936 * h1_2 + 2.2980125 * h1_3 + 0.11725044 * h1_4 + 0.38761032 * h1_5 + 0.58688897 * h1_6 + 1.9527504 * h1_7 - 0.2165651  # output 2
    return argmax(o0, o1, o2)
```

```python
# Decision list over two piecewise-linear boundaries (each boundary = AND of two half-planes).
# Inputs look like the Iris measurements: sepal_length sepal length, sepal_width sepal width,
# petal_length petal length, petal_width petal width (cm). Classes: 0 small petals, 1 middle, 2 large petals.
def net(sepal_length, sepal_width, petal_length, petal_width):
    # class 2: large petal size relative to sepal size; two half-planes (the net's boundary is curved)
    big_a = 12 * petal_length + 24 * petal_width - 3 * sepal_length - 11 * sepal_width
    big_b = 11 * petal_length + 19 * petal_width - 5 * sepal_length - 6 * sepal_width
    if big_a > 50.4 and big_b > 38.2:
        return 2
    # class 0: small petals relative to sepals; two half-planes
    small_a = 6 * (petal_length + petal_width) - 2 * sepal_length - 3 * sepal_width
    small_b = 17 * petal_length + 19 * petal_width - 4 * sepal_length - 11 * sepal_width
    if small_a < 0.7 and small_b < 3.36:
        return 0
    return 1
```

```python
    h1_1 = sigmoid(-1.9822463 * sepal_length + 1.3831801 * sepal_width + 1.244342 * petal_length + 0.104599506 * petal_width + 1.1060718)

    h1_5 = sigmoid(0.59359187 * sepal_length + 2.5276554 * sepal_width - 3.3193276 * petal_length - 4.471064 * petal_width + 1.8953542)
    h1_6 = sigmoid(-0.6408659 * sepal_length - 1.1644258 * sepal_width + 2.2058144 * petal_length + 2.282884 * petal_width - 0.42685923)

    o1 = 2.7581549 * h1_0 + 1.1703169 * h1_1 - 6.5444956 * h1_2 - 4.331185 * h1_3 + 4.4670296 * h1_4 - 1.92572 * h1_5 + 4.610048 * h1_6 - 3.221034 * h1_7 + 1.4081368
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the net is

A 4 -> 8 tanh -> 3 argmax classifier. The input bounds (4.3-7.9, 2.0-4.4, 1.0-6.9, 0.1-2.5) match the
Iris measurements: x0 sepal length, x1 sepal width, x2 petal length, x3 petal width. This is a guess from
the ranges only. Over the box the net predicts class 0 for 22% of points, class 1 for 59% and class 2 for
19%. Class 0 means small petals relative to the sepals and class 2 means large petals.

#### What I changed

- **Dropped the tanh layer.** Refitting the output layer with one hidden unit left out still gave
  100 to 3700 confident errors for 7 of the 8 units. So a smaller tanh net would not have read much
  better.
- **Rewrote the net as a decision list.** It checks class 2 first, then class 0, else returns 1.
  - Class 2 vs {0,1} and class 0 vs 2 are clean.
  - Class 0 vs 1 and class 2 vs 1 are *not* linearly separable on the net's confident points (an LP
    gave a negative margin). One half-plane is not enough for either boundary; each one is curved.
- **Fitted each boundary as an AND of two half-planes.** I alternated LPs: give each negative point to
  the half-plane that rejects it most, then solve a max-margin LP per half-plane. Confident points are
  those with top-2 gap ≥ 0.636; points in the unsure zone are free.
  - Two half-planes are enough for both boundaries.
  - A third half-plane barely raised the margin (0.02 → 0.03 in x3 units), so the curvature is gentle
    and spread out.
- **Snapped the coefficients by search.** For each half-plane I made the coefficient of one input 1 and
  put the others on a grid (steps 1, 1/2, 1/3, 1/4, 1/5), within 5° of the LP direction. I kept the pairs
  that still separate every confident point, plus the band of feasible thresholds. I then scaled to
  integer coefficients and put each threshold mid-band.
  - The margins are thin (about 0.01-0.03 in units of x3), so fully "nice" coefficients like
    `x2 + x3 > c` fail. The coefficients that survived are small integers (up to 24).
- **Checked robustness.** I fitted on the checker's input sets for seeds 0-3 together. Seeds 4-7 were
  only used to choose between the remaining candidates, and the final program has 0 confident
  disagreements on all of them. One class-2 pair that passed seeds 0-3 failed on seeds 4-6 (1-3 points),
  and I dropped it.

Result: `PASS (snap)`, with 0 disagreements where the net is confident. About 2% of inputs differ, all in
the unsure zone, all of them points next to a curved boundary.

```
class 2 if 12*x2 + 24*x3 - 3*x0 - 11*x1 > 50.4 and 11*x2 + 19*x3 - 5*x0 - 6*x1 > 38.2
class 0 if 6*(x2 + x3) - 2*x0 - 3*x1 < 0.7  and 17*x2 + 19*x3 - 4*x0 - 11*x1 < 3.36
else class 1
```

How to read it:
- **Class 0:** the petal size (x2 + x3, weighted by 6) is small compared with the sepal size
  (2*x0 + 3*x1).
- **Class 2:** petal size, with petal width weighted about twice as much as length, is large compared
  with the sepal size.
- **Sepals push against both:** for the same petals, larger sepals push toward class 1 (from class 2)
  and toward class 0 (from class 1).

#### Heuristics

- For argmax nets on continuous boxes, first find out which class pairs share a boundary. Then fit a
  decision list: the class that is cleanly separable from the others goes last or first, and each test
  only has to separate the classes that are still left.
- Fit each boundary only on points outside the checker's unsure zone, using the checker's own
  definition: top-2 gap ≥ margin_frac × p95. Points inside the zone are free, and that slack is what
  makes a simple rule possible.
- Test linear separability exactly, with an LP that maximises the margin and fixes one coefficient to
  ±1. Logistic or SVM fits can hide a separator that exists. If the LP margin is negative, the boundary
  is curved.
- For a curved boundary, try an AND/OR of 2 half-planes, fitted by alternating LPs: assign each
  negative point to the half-plane that rejects it most, then re-solve. Initialise by splitting on the
  coordinate where a split makes both halves separable (testing splits at quantiles of each input
  finds it quickly). Adding a 3rd piece rarely pays off.
- Snap with a feasibility search instead of rounding. Normalise by each input in turn, put the other
  coefficients on 1, 1/2, 1/3, 1/4, 1/5 grids around the LP direction, and keep the ones that still
  separate. The threshold then has a feasible interval, so choose a round value inside it. Then
  multiply through to get integer coefficients.
- Bounded inputs with tanh/sigmoid units: the margins across the whole box can be thin (about 1% of an
  input's range), much thinner than on real data. Check the final rule on several fresh input sets
  (other seeds of the checker's sampler), and choose between snapped candidates by their worst case
  on sets they were not fitted on.
- Recognising the dataset from the input bounds helps with naming, but the rule must still match the
  net everywhere in the box, including unrealistic corners (for example a tiny sepal with a huge petal
  width). Most of the curvature that blocks a single-line rule was in those corners.

</details>

```python
# dumped from a neural network: 4 inputs -> 8 -> 3 outputs
# inputs: sepal_length: float [4.3, 7.9], sepal_width: float [2, 4.4], petal_length: float [1, 6.9], petal_width: float [0.1, 2.5]
# output: argmax over 3 outputs: the returned class index
def net(sepal_length, sepal_width, petal_length, petal_width):
    h1_0 = tanh(-0.10328401 * sepal_length - 0.93804854 * sepal_width + 1.205398 * petal_length + 1.1017983 * petal_width - 0.62621504)  # layer 1 neuron 0
    h1_1 = tanh(0.3068952 * sepal_length + 0.26014033 * sepal_width - 0.25302812 * petal_length + 0.157196 * petal_width - 0.8039045)  # layer 1 neuron 1
    h1_2 = tanh(0.07346287 * sepal_length - 1.209415 * sepal_width + 1.1402633 * petal_length + 1.17725 * petal_width - 0.61004764)  # layer 1 neuron 2
    h1_3 = tanh(-0.6446873 * sepal_length - 0.039794564 * sepal_width + 1.0279653 * petal_length + 1.5032302 * petal_width - 2.635843)  # layer 1 neuron 3
    h1_4 = tanh(0.09278318 * sepal_length - 0.9181576 * sepal_width + 0.51381034 * petal_length + 1.236799 * petal_width - 1.9624379)  # layer 1 neuron 4
    h1_5 = tanh(0.22067791 * sepal_length + 0.69114584 * sepal_width - 0.7694072 * petal_length - 1.3908933 * petal_width + 2.594415)  # layer 1 neuron 5
    h1_6 = tanh(0.052807882 * sepal_length + 0.9291967 * sepal_width - 1.012673 * petal_length - 1.3334575 * petal_width + 0.56891435)  # layer 1 neuron 6
    h1_7 = tanh(-0.5038638 * sepal_length - 0.46178854 * sepal_width - 0.6737536 * petal_length - 0.54024696 * petal_width - 0.4957189)  # layer 1 neuron 7
    o0 = -1.4565178 * h1_0 + 0.13072819 * h1_1 - 1.4791877 * h1_2 - 0.9930608 * h1_3 - 1.0011948 * h1_4 + 1.4661798 * h1_5 + 1.4057355 * h1_6 - 0.47365838 * h1_7 + 0.80177355  # output 0
    o1 = 2.0900943 * h1_0 - 0.34827912 * h1_1 + 2.2177727 * h1_2 - 2.580952 * h1_3 - 1.1436911 * h1_4 + 3.1188242 * h1_5 - 2.1326165 * h1_6 + 0.17687081 * h1_7 - 0.19921179  # output 1
    o2 = 0.37263536 * h1_0 + 0.2130597 * h1_1 + 0.64867395 * h1_2 + 2.6320455 * h1_3 + 1.880635 * h1_4 - 2.9293497 * h1_5 - 0.3924201 * h1_6 + 0.4740209 * h1_7 - 0.01744657  # output 2
    return argmax(o0, o1, o2)
```

```python
# Refactored from dump.py: 4 inputs, 3 classes.  The input ranges match the Iris measurements
# (sepal_length, sepal_width sepal-like; petal_length, petal_width petal-like), so the units are named after what they compare.
# Scores are relative to class 1 (class 1 has score 0).
def net(sepal_length, sepal_width, petal_length, petal_width):
    # main gate: petal_length + petal_width large relative to sepal_width -> not class 0 (merge of 3 near-duplicate units)
    petal_vs_x1 = tanh(-0.03 * sepal_length - 1.03 * sepal_width + 1.12 * petal_length + 1.2 * petal_width - 0.6)
    # class-2 detectors: petal_length, petal_width large relative to sepal_length / sepal_width
    petal_vs_x0 = tanh(-0.65 * sepal_length + 1.02 * petal_length + 1.5 * petal_width - 2.5)
    x3_vs_x1 = tanh(0.1 * sepal_length - 0.9 * sepal_width + 0.5 * petal_length + 1.25 * petal_width - 2)
    # class-1 detector: petal_length, petal_width small
    small_petal = tanh(0.22 * sepal_length + 0.7 * sepal_width - 0.77 * petal_length - 1.4 * petal_width + 2.6)
    score0 = 1.5 - 11 * petal_vs_x1 + 2 * petal_vs_x0 - 1.5 * small_petal
    score2 = 5 * petal_vs_x0 + 3 * x3_vs_x1 - 5 * petal_vs_x1 - 6 * small_petal
    return argmax(score0, 0, score2)
```

```python
    o2 = 0.7452707 * h1_0 + 0.4261194 * h1_1 + 1.2973479 * h1_2 + 5.264091 * h1_3 + 3.76127 * h1_4 - 5.8586993 * h1_5 - 0.7848402 * h1_6 - 2.91674654994001
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

Result: `agent.py` gives PASS (snap) against the net, with 0 disagreements where the net is confident.
It also had 0 confident disagreements on my own 2.4M-point validation set (uniform points, face/corner-biased
points and a 25^4 grid). The dump itself is only PASS (exact) within the unsure zone: float64 vs float32
already flips about 870 points there.

#### What I changed

1. **Constant unit.** The pre-activation of unit 7 is in [-12.4, -4.5] over the whole box, so tanh is -1
   there. I folded it into the output biases.
2. **Dead unit.** Unit 1 has small output weights. Setting it to 0 changed no confident decision on 1.66M
   test points, so I removed it.
3. **Near-duplicate units merged.** Units 0, 2 and -6 (unit 6 negated, because tanh is odd) all compute
   about tanh(-x1 + x2 + x3 - 0.6), and their output weights are in proportion. I replaced them with
   one unit: the mean of the three affine rows, with the summed output weights. That left 4 tanh units.
4. **Scores relative to class 1.** Argmax only depends on score differences, so I subtracted o1 and
   return `argmax(score0, 0, score2)`. That removes one output row, and unit `x3_vs_x1` drops out of
   score0 entirely (its weight there was 0.14).
5. **Snapping.** I snapped greedily, trying coarse steps first (1, 0.5, 0.25, 0.1, 0.05, 0.01) for every
   weight. I kept a snap only if a pool of 0.5M confident points near the boundary still had 0 errors.
   Output weights became small integers or halves: -11, 2, -1.5, 1.5 / -5, 5, 3, -6.
   Unit weights ended on 2-decimal values.
6. **Tried and rejected: pure linear rules.**
   - Class 0 vs the rest is almost a halfspace, about x2 + 1.05 x3 < 0.945 x1 + 0.45.
   - Class 2 vs class 1 is close to the AND of two halfspaces.
   - The LP max-margin was only about 0.003-0.008, measured in x3 units. A single class-0 line already
     failed at box corners: the boundary bends where the dominant unit saturates. That left no room to
     snap, and it would have needed 4+ lines with 4-decimal coefficients.
   - The 4-unit tanh form is shorter and snaps much better.

Reading of the program: score0 is driven almost entirely by `-11 * petal_vs_x1`. Class 0 is "x2 + x3
small relative to x1". Class 2 vs 1 is mainly `small_petal` and `petal_vs_x0`, which is "x2 and x3
large", with the bar raised for large x0 and x1. If the inputs are the Iris measurements, these are
setosa / versicolor / virginica.

#### Heuristics

- Check each unit's pre-activation range over the input box first. A unit saturated over the whole box
  (|z| > ~4 for tanh/sigmoid) is a constant; fold it into the next layer's bias.
- Ablation test: set a unit to 0, or to its mean, and count confident disagreements on a large pool. Units
  with small outgoing weights often drop out with no change.
- For odd activations (tanh), compare weight rows up to sign: w and -w are duplicates. When normalized rows
  and outgoing-weight ratios agree, merge them into one unit (mean row, summed outgoing weights). Try it
  even when rows differ by ~20%; argmax decisions tolerate it.
- For argmax outputs, subtract one class's score from all of them: `argmax(s0, 0, s2)`. One fewer row, and
  units whose weight differences are ~0 disappear from a score.
- Before trying to fit linear rules, check how much slack the unsure zone allows: run an LP max-margin
  separator on confident points. If the margin is tiny, piecewise-linear rules will not snap. Keep the
  activation form and snap its weights instead.
- Build the test pool with box-face/corner-biased samples and points bisected onto the net's class
  boundaries, not just uniform samples. Simple rules failed first at corners.
- Snap greedily, coarse step first, accepting only zero confident errors. Snap output weights first
  (they become integers easily), then unit rows. Validate on a fresh independent pool afterwards.

</details>

```python
def net(sepal_length, sepal_width, petal_length, petal_width):
    # six tanh features; the score of class 1 is fixed at 0, classes 0 and 2 are scored against it
    small = tanh(0.325 * sepal_length + 1.3 * sepal_width - 1.8 * petal_length - 2.4 * petal_width + 1.2)       # high when petal_length, petal_width are small
    big_a = tanh(0.1 * sepal_length - 1.2 * sepal_width + 0.7 * petal_length + 0.9 * petal_width - 1.6)
    big_b = tanh(-0.2 * sepal_length - 0.7 * sepal_width + 0.7 * petal_length + 1.4 * petal_width - 2.6)       # high when petal_width is large
    big_c = tanh(-0.6 * sepal_length - 0.45 * sepal_width + 1.1 * petal_length + 1.4 * petal_width - 2.1)
    long_x2 = tanh(-0.1 * sepal_length - 0.9 * sepal_width + 1.4 * petal_length + 0.6 * petal_width - 1)      # mostly petal_length against sepal_width
    narrow_x1 = tanh(0.15 * sepal_length - 1.3 * sepal_width + 0.2 * petal_length - 0.4 * petal_width + 1.9)   # high when sepal_width is small
    score0 = 5.5 * small + 0.75 * big_b + 1.25 * big_c - 4 * long_x2 - 2.25 * narrow_x1 - 0.25
    score2 = 2 * small + 3.5 * big_a + 6.5 * big_b + 4.75 * big_c - 2 * long_x2 - 2.75 * narrow_x1 - 1
    return argmax(score0, 0, score2)
```

```python
    h1_4 = sigmoid(-1.164275 * sepal_length - 0.78490084 * sepal_width + 2.1634836 * petal_length + 2.6279647 * petal_width - 4.368646)
    h1_5 = sigmoid(-0.12009965 * sepal_length - 1.9113783 * sepal_width + 2.8838606 * petal_length + 1.1719308 * petal_width - 2.0757189)
    h1_6 = sigmoid(0.23689257 * sepal_length - 2.4743984 * sepal_width + 0.40863472 * petal_length - 0.887645 * petal_width + 3.9744458)
    h1_7 = sigmoid(-0.4784385 * sepal_length - 2.2105744 * sepal_width + 1.7590352 * petal_length + 3.1156714 * petal_width - 3.8039057)
    o0 = 7.0179186 * h1_0 - 1.9025813 * h1_1 - 2.7993236 * h1_2 - 1.7465461 * h1_4 - 2.9135933 * h1_5 - 1.354841 * h1_6 - 2.8635125 * h1_7 + 3.49859878327297
    o1 = -4.1426272 * h1_0 - 1.2009593 * h1_1 - 3.8331835 * h1_2 - 3.8313026 * h1_4 + 4.5639215 * h1_5 + 2.9917486 * h1_6 - 3.6503026 * h1_7 + 5.166692720954018
    o2 = 0.17493366 * h1_0 + 3.4732585 * h1_1 + 4.6747236 * h1_2 + 4.555492 * h1_4 + 0.77958524 * h1_5 - 2.4354951 * h1_6 + 3.697789 * h1_7 - 8.000733777550002
    return argmax(o0, o1, o2)
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network is

4 float inputs, 8 tanh hidden units, 3 outputs, argmax. The input ranges (x0 in [4.3, 7.9],
x1 in [2, 4.4], x2 in [1, 6.9], x3 in [0.1, 2.5]) look like a classic 3-class measurement dataset.
I did not use that guess for anything. Feature names are taken from the weights only.

#### What I changed

1. **Constant unit removed.** Over the whole input box, hidden unit 3's pre-activation lies in
   [-11.0, -4.0], so `tanh` is -1 everywhere (error below 7e-4). I folded it into the output biases.
2. **Scores taken relative to class 1.** Argmax depends only on differences, so I return
   `argmax(score0, 0, score2)` with `score0 = o0 - o1` and `score2 = o2 - o1`. That removes one
   output row.
3. **Removed one more unit (7 to 6 live units) by merge and refit.** Old units 2 and 7 point in
   almost the same direction (cosine 0.99, activation correlation 0.97). I dropped unit 7,
   re-solved the output weights by least squares on the other units' activations, then refit all
   weights with a decision-aware loss. Removing any one of the other units this way left confident
   disagreements, and so did a 5-unit refit (it stalled at 6–10 confident disagreements).
4. **Snapped every coefficient with snap-and-refit.** I snapped one coefficient at a time, closest
   to the grid first: first layer to multiples of 0.1, output layer to multiples of 0.25. After
   each snap I refit the coefficients still free, and kept the snap only if no confident
   disagreement was left. Three leftovers needed a finer step (0.05 / 0.025: `0.325`, `-0.45`,
   `-0.7`). One output weight snapped to 0, so `big_a` only feeds `score2`.
5. **Named the features by their dominant inputs:**
   - `small`: x2 and x3 low. It is the main evidence for class 0.
   - `big_a`, `big_b`, `big_c`: x2 and x3 high. They are the evidence for class 2.
   - `long_x2`: x2 large compared with x1.
   - `narrow_x1`: x1 small.

   `long_x2` and `narrow_x1` lower both scores, so they favour class 1.

**Result:** `PASS (snap)`, with 0 confident disagreements. The 4815 disagreements are all inside
the unsure zone, which covers 3% of the domain. I also checked two fresh input sets built the same
way with other seeds (11 and 12, about 237k points each): 0 confident disagreements in both.
`PASS (exact)` cannot be reached once the coefficients are snapped, because tanh outputs move
continuously.

I also tried distilling into a smaller tanh net from scratch with plain MSE (2–7 units). It
reached the logits roughly but always left hundreds to thousands of confident disagreements. A
random start is much worse than starting from the original net's weights.

#### Heuristics

- **Saturated units.** Check every hidden unit's pre-activation range over the whole input box.
  If |z| > 4 everywhere, tanh/sigmoid is a constant. Fold it into the next layer's bias.
- **Argmax nets.** Subtract one output from all the others (fix a reference class at 0). This
  removes one score row and shows which features separate which pair of classes.
- **Merge candidates.** Look for hidden units whose weight vectors have |cosine| > 0.95. Units
  that are nearly parallel, or anti-parallel with a different bias, are redundant.
- **Remove units by re-solving the output layer.** Drop a unit, solve the output layer by least
  squares on the remaining activations, then fine-tune.
  - Rank the units to try by the disagreement count right after the least-squares step.
  - The unit with the fewest disagreements is usually the removable one. It is not always the
    unit with the smallest output weight.
- **Decision-aware refit loss.** Use a small MSE on the score differences plus a squared hinge.
  The hinge asks the target class to beat the others by about half the net's own top-2 gap, and
  only at points where the net is confident. Plain MSE distillation leaves boundary errors that
  the checker counts.
- **Snap one coefficient at a time.**
  - Snap the coefficient closest to the grid first, refit the free ones, and keep the snap only
    if the check still has zero confident disagreements.
  - Joint quantization-aware training (straight-through rounding) on a 0.1 grid oscillated
    badly; one-at-a-time snapping was far more reliable.
  - The last few coefficients often need a finer grid (0.05 / 0.025) because nothing is left free
    to compensate.
- **Re-check on fresh points.** Re-check the final program on input sets with other random seeds,
  to make sure the refit did not overfit the checker's own sample.
- **Expected outcome for small tanh classifiers.** A full symbolic rewrite (linear rules, a
  decision list) does not hold over the whole input box. The pairwise boundaries agree with a
  linear fit on only about 98% of points. Keeping the tanh features and simplifying their
  coefficients is the realistic target.

</details>

```python
    h1_1 = tanh(-0.58114344 * sepal_length - 0.46891388 * sepal_width + 1.0732538 * petal_length + 1.0241663 * petal_width - 1.2670499)  # layer 1 neuron 1
    h1_2 = tanh(0.041967154 * sepal_length + 0.9541782 * sepal_width - 1.0709416 * petal_length - 1.3665671 * petal_width + 0.64097065)  # layer 1 neuron 2
    h1_3 = tanh(-0.082248084 * sepal_length - 0.5536739 * sepal_width + 0.444013 * petal_length + 1.282096 * petal_width - 2.570428)  # layer 1 neuron 3
    h1_4 = tanh(0.65923417 * sepal_length + 0.009036263 * sepal_width - 0.9381791 * petal_length - 1.3239202 * petal_width + 1.9895769)  # layer 1 neuron 4
    h1_5 = tanh(-0.18934911 * sepal_length - 0.22305197 * sepal_width + 0.52741957 * petal_length + 0.8407295 * petal_width - 1.5697554)  # layer 1 neuron 5
    h1_6 = tanh(0.62481517 * sepal_length + 0.2394252 * sepal_width + 0.455872 * petal_length + 0.37898442 * petal_width + 0.60163647)  # layer 1 neuron 6
    h1_7 = tanh(-0.004276656 * sepal_length - 0.93783766 * sepal_width + 0.7214577 * petal_length + 1.0667368 * petal_width - 1.977088)  # layer 1 neuron 7
    o0 = 1.684243 * h1_0 - 0.65362304 * h1_1 + 1.9596672 * h1_2 - 1.8341517 * h1_3 + 0.93111944 * h1_4 - 1.6702591 * h1_5 + 0.26156145 * h1_6 - 1.6586988 * h1_7 + 0.111238725  # output 0
    o1 = -2.5106354 * h1_0 - 0.19267826 * h1_1 - 2.6470964 * h1_2 - 2.1927223 * h1_3 + 2.1930623 * h1_4 - 0.38061708 * h1_5 - 0.012486145 * h1_6 - 1.2908747 * h1_7 + 0.5580896  # output 1
    o2 = -0.094940275 * h1_0 + 1.5557004 * h1_1 - 0.26529682 * h1_2 + 3.0262375 * h1_3 - 2.3108373 * h1_4 + 2.4744735 * h1_5 - 0.6713576 * h1_6 + 2.5582798 * h1_7 - 0.19190548  # output 2
```

```python
def net(sepal_length, sepal_width, petal_length, petal_width):
    # class 0: petal_width below a plane
    if petal_width + 5 / 6 * petal_length < 0.125 * sepal_length + 0.625 * sepal_width + 0.37:
        return 0
    # class 2 vs 1: petal_width above a plane bent by a parabola in u
    u = sepal_width - 0.65 * sepal_length + 0.15 * petal_length
    if petal_width > 2.1 + 0.18 * sepal_length + 0.43 * sepal_width - 0.56 * petal_length + u * u / 24:
        return 2
    return 1
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network does

4 inputs -> 8 tanh units -> 3 scores, argmax. Over the input box:

- Unit 6 is saturated at +1 everywhere (smallest value about 0.99995), so it is a constant bias.
- Classes 0 and 2 never touch. No sampled point lies within score 1.0 of a 0/2 tie, so the
  decision is a two-step list: class 0 against the rest, then class 2 against class 1.
- The class 0 / class 1 boundary depends on units 0 and 2 only (the others are saturated there).
  It is a hyperplane: a linear SVM on confident points separates it with no errors.
- The class 1 / class 2 boundary depends on units 1, 3, 4, 5 and 7 (units 0, 2 and 6 are saturated
  at -1, -1 and +1 there). It is curved. The best plane, fit by minimax, misses by up to 4.4x the
  unsure-zone half-width (about 0.03 in x3). A full quadratic fits. Its quadratic part is close to
  rank 1: one eigenvalue is 0.063, along the direction (-0.54, 0.83, 0.13) in (x0, x1, x2), and the
  rest are 10x smaller. So the boundary is a plane bent by one parabola.

#### Result (`agent.py`)

```
if x3 + 5/6*x2 < 0.125*x0 + 0.625*x1 + 0.37:            -> 0
u = x1 - 0.65*x0 + 0.15*x2
if x3 > 2.1 + 0.18*x0 + 0.43*x1 - 0.56*x2 + u*u/24:      -> 2
else                                                     -> 1
```

`check.py` gives PASS (snap): 0 disagreements where the net is confident. 4599 disagreements fall in
the unsure zone, which covers 3.09% of the domain; the dump itself has 1042 there because of float
ties. On 2 x 3M fresh uniform points, the largest net margin at a disagreement was 0.44, under the
0.486 zone limit. I did not reach PASS (exact). The net's boundaries are smooth tanh curves, so any
short formula differs from it in a thin band.

#### How I got there

1. Sampled 1M uniform points. For each, recorded the class and margin (top1 - top2), and checked
   which units are saturated, both overall and near each pairwise boundary.
2. Bisected along x3 to get exact boundary points: about 127k for 0/1 and about 105k for 1/2. At
   each point I computed the zone half-width in x3, as zone margin / (d score / d x3).
3. Fit the boundary as `x3 = f(x0, x1, x2)` by minimax linear programming on the residual divided by
   that half-width. A form passes when the max is below 1.
4. 0/1: the plane fits with a max of 0.49. Snapped coefficients (1/8, 5/8, -5/6) keep the max at
   0.86. Integer-coefficient searches were worse (best 0.77, with x3 coefficient 17), so I kept the
   simple fractions.
5. 1/2: plane 4.4, rank-1 parabola in (x0, x1) only 1.3, rank-1 parabola in (x0, x1, x2) 0.53
   (a = 0.65, d = 0.15). Adding x2^2 did not help (its coefficient fit to about 0). Coefficients
   snapped to 2 digits and 1/24 give about 0.85-0.94.

#### Heuristics

- **Check saturation first.** A unit that is constant over the whole box (here |tanh| > 0.9999) is
  a bias; fold it in.
- **Analyze each pairwise boundary separately.** Look for class pairs that never become top-2
  together (no point near their tie). That turns an argmax into a short decision list.
- **Find the active units at each boundary.** Restrict to points near a boundary and list which
  hidden units are unsaturated there. Each boundary usually involves only a subset of units, which
  tells you how complex it can be.
- **Measure the error budget in input units.** Bisect onto the boundary along one input. Divide the
  zone margin by the score gradient along that input to get the allowed error per boundary point.
  Then fit `x_k = f(others)` by minimax LP on residual / half-width. A max below 1 means it will pass.
  This is far more predictive than counting misclassified samples.
- **Read curvature off the Hessian.** If a plane is not enough, fit a full quadratic and
  eigen-decompose its quadratic part. A dominant eigenvalue means "plane + c*u^2" with u along that
  eigenvector: a few readable terms instead of 10. Grid-search u's direction and solve the rest by
  LP.
- **Check snaps with the same minimax metric.** Try simple fractions first. Integer-coefficient
  searches over small multiples rarely beat 2-digit fractions when the margin budget is tight.
- **Check the dump first.** Even the exact dump can disagree in the unsure zone (float32 ties), so
  only confident disagreements matter.

</details>

```python
    h1_1 = tanh(0.6959892 * sepal_length + 0.20894586 * sepal_width - 1.1872332 * petal_length - 1.1590441 * petal_width + 2.135364)  # layer 1 neuron 1

    h1_3 = tanh(-0.58668375 * sepal_length - 0.41873977 * sepal_width + 1.0619329 * petal_length + 1.2863941 * petal_width - 1.8344276)  # layer 1 neuron 3
    h1_4 = tanh(0.29849476 * sepal_length + 0.5259834 * sepal_width - 1.2500339 * petal_length - 0.5341687 * petal_width + 0.54961765)  # layer 1 neuron 4
    h1_5 = tanh(0.5256733 * sepal_length + 0.43981478 * sepal_width - 1.3827968 * petal_length - 0.74056226 * petal_width + 0.1010675)  # layer 1 neuron 5
    h1_6 = tanh(0.69014716 * sepal_length - 0.19924058 * sepal_width + 0.26904312 * petal_length + 0.48038366 * petal_width + 0.15208726)  # layer 1 neuron 6
    h1_7 = tanh(0.18827228 * sepal_length + 0.67075783 * sepal_width - 0.61952984 * petal_length - 1.6120139 * petal_width + 2.915745)  # layer 1 neuron 7
    o0 = -1.1567224 * h1_0 + 1.1817411 * h1_1 - 0.7133858 * h1_2 - 0.9546134 * h1_3 + 2.0846865 * h1_4 + 2.009265 * h1_5 + 1.1523964 * h1_6 + 1.2398436 * h1_7 + 0.5627424  # output 0

    o2 = 0.60844964 * h1_0 - 2.747296 * h1_1 - 1.1912255 * h1_2 + 2.3948326 * h1_3 - 0.71212405 * h1_4 - 0.7771166 * h1_5 - 0.43877214 * h1_6 - 3.2724493 * h1_7 - 0.3485704  # output 2
```

```python
def net(sepal_length, sepal_width, petal_length, petal_width):
    # The input bounds (sepal_length 4.3..7.9, sepal_width 2..4.4, petal_length 1..6.9, petal_width 0.1..2.5) look like the four Iris
    # measurements (sepal length, sepal width, petal length, petal width); petal_length and petal_width dominate.
    # Five soft steps (tanh) on snapped linear forms; the net's 8 units were reduced to these:
    #   one unit was always saturated (constant), two near-parallel pairs were merged.
    small = tanh(0.4 * sepal_length + 0.5 * sepal_width - 1.3 * petal_length - 0.6 * petal_width + 0.3)        # +1 when petal_length, petal_width are small
    large = tanh(petal_length + 1.2 * petal_width - 0.8 * sepal_width - 0.8)                          # +1 when petal_length, petal_width are large
    short = tanh(0.6 * sepal_width + 0.6 * petal_length - 0.1 * petal_width - sepal_length + 1.2)               # +1 when sepal_length is small
    mid = tanh(0.6 * sepal_length + 0.3 * sepal_width - 1.1 * petal_length - 1.2 * petal_width + 2)            # -1 when petal_length, petal_width are large
    thin = tanh(0.2 * sepal_length + 0.7 * sepal_width - 0.6 * petal_length - 1.6 * petal_width + 2.9)         # -1 when petal_width is large
    # class 0: mainly "small" petal_length/petal_width
    if 20 * small - 7 * large - 5 * short > 6:
        return 0
    # class 2 vs 1: large petal_length/petal_width, mostly through petal_width
    if 20 * mid + 12 * thin + 4 * short < -9:
        return 2
    return 1
```

AST tool (no LLM, `ast4`):

```python
    h1_5 = min(max(0.262836625 * sepal_length + 0.2199073875 * sepal_width - 0.691398375 * petal_length - 0.370281125 * petal_width + 0.55053375, 0), 1)
    h1_7 = min(max(0.09413614 * sepal_length + 0.335378925 * sepal_width - 0.309764925 * petal_length - 0.806006975 * petal_width + 1.9578725, 0), 1)
    o0 = -2.3134449 * h1_0 + 2.3634822 * h1_1 - 1.4267716 * h1_2 - 1.9092268 * h1_3 + 4.169373 * h1_4 + 4.01853 * h1_5 + 2.4796872 * h1_7 - 1.9758717160575845
    o1 = 3.4980695 * h1_0 + 4.359905 * h1_1 + 2.6973822 * h1_2 - 3.7255242 * h1_3 - 3.3873613 * h1_4 - 3.9326885 * h1_5 + 5.8421974 * h1_7 - 2.0790290628783135
    o2 = 1.2168993 * h1_0 - 5.494592 * h1_1 - 2.382451 * h1_2 + 4.789665 * h1_3 - 1.4242481 * h1_4 - 1.5542332 * h1_5 - 6.5448985 * h1_7 + 4.909661408784217
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

Net: 4 inputs -> 8 tanh units -> 3 scores, argmax. Result: `agent.py`, PASS (snap). It has 0 confident
disagreements on the check set, and also 0 on 14 other input-set seeds (1-18). Every disagreement lies in the
net's unsure zone (score gap < 0.584).

#### What I changed and why

1. **Constant unit removed.** Unit 6's pre-activation is 3.5..7.4 over the whole box, so tanh is 0.988..1.0. I
   folded it into the output biases.
2. **Near-parallel units merged.**
   - Units 4 and 5 have nearly the same direction and nearly equal output weights (D0: 3.78 / 3.98), so I
     replaced them with one tanh of the averaged pre-activation.
   - Unit 1 and the *negated* unit 3 are near-parallel too (tanh is odd, so `-tanh(-z)` = `tanh(z)`), and their
     output weights have opposite signs. I merged them the same way.

   This leaves 5 units, still with 0 confident errors.
3. **Scores to a decision list.** I rewrote the scores relative to class 1 (s0 = o0-o1, s2 = o2-o1). Where the net
   is confident, s0 > 0 and s2 > 0 never hold together. So `if s0>0: 0 elif s2>0: 2 else 1` matches, each test is
   an independent binary decision, and each test's scale is free.
4. **Unused units dropped per decision.** I refitted the output weights with an LP (maximise the margin over the
   confident points near the boundary, then verify on all confident points). The class-0 test needs only 3 units
   (small, large, short), and the class-2 test needs only 3 (mid, thin, short). Removing any one of them is
   infeasible.
5. **Hidden weights snapped**, one unit at a time on a 0.1 grid (and 0.2 where it still fit), with the output LP
   re-solved after each snap. For robustness I fitted on a pool of 10 input-set seeds (2.2M confident points)
   rather than only the checker's seed. A version tuned on seed 0 alone failed by 1-4 points on other seeds.
6. **Output coefficients snapped** by a grid search around the LP solution. I picked the fewest-decimals
   solution with 0 errors on the whole pool, then scaled it to integers: `20a - 7b - 5c > 6` and
   `20d + 12e + 4c < -9`.

What was not possible:
- A purely linear rule. The LP is infeasible for class 0 vs rest, for 2 vs 1, and for 3 linear scores.
- A 2-unit tanh model per decision. I trained it from several starts: 4 and 29 errors at best, with sharp,
  ugly weights.
- PASS exact, which would need the full net.

Reading the program: the bounds look like the Iris measurements. Class 0 = small x2/x3 (petal). Classes 1/2 are
split mainly by x3, then x2, with a small x0/x1 correction (unit "short").

#### Heuristics

- **Constant units:** check each unit's pre-activation range over the whole input box. A tanh/sigmoid unit with
  |z| > ~3.5 everywhere is a constant; fold it into the bias.
- **Merging candidates:** look for pairs of hidden units with near-parallel weight vectors *and* similar output
  weights. Also compare against the negated vector: for tanh, `-tanh(-z)` = `tanh(z)`, so a unit and a
  sign-flipped unit with opposite output weights merge. Replace a pair by one unit on the averaged pre-activation,
  with the summed output weight. Test with a confident-point mismatch count.
- **Argmax nets:** subtract a reference class's score and check whether the positive regions of the differences
  overlap where the net is confident. If they don't, the argmax becomes a decision list of independent binary
  tests, each with a free scale. That makes snapping much easier.
- **Feasibility by LP:** with the hidden features fixed, refitting the output weights is a linear feasibility
  problem: maximise the margin t subject to sign constraints on the confident points. Use only the points near
  the boundary (gap < a few margins) for speed, then verify on all confident points. The LP also tells which
  units each decision needs (drop a feature, see if t stays > 0), and whether a purely linear rule exists.
- **Snap with re-solve:** snap one unit at a time on a coarse-to-fine grid (0.5, 0.25, 0.2, 0.1), re-solve the
  output LP after each snap, and keep the coarsest grid that is still feasible.
- **Don't overfit the check set:** the checker's input set is fixed, and a rule tuned on it can fail by a few
  points on other random sets. Fit and snap on a pool of several input-set seeds, and verify on held-out seeds.
- **Output snapping:** grid-search the remaining output coefficients (step 0.01) around the LP solution, rank by
  number of decimals then by distance to the LP centre, and scale the winner to integers.
- **Expect thin unsure zones on smooth nets:** with tanh nets the boundaries are curved. If a linear or 2-unit
  rule is infeasible, keep a few soft-step units rather than force a piecewise rule.

</details>

```python
def net(petal_length, petal_width):
    # two straight decision lines split the (petal_length, petal_width) box into three bands
    low = petal_length + 3.3 * petal_width < 4.9      # small petal_length and small petal_width -> class 0
    high = petal_length + 1.1 * petal_width > 6.8     # large petal_length and large petal_width -> class 2
    if low:
        return 0
    if high:
        return 2
    return 1
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the net does
2 inputs (x0 in [1, 6.9], x1 in [0.1, 2.5]), 8 tanh units, argmax over 3 classes.
Printing the class on a 60x25 grid showed three bands separated by two nearly straight lines;
class 0 and class 2 never touch.

#### What I changed
- I bisected the class change along x0 for each x1 from 0.1 to 2.5 (step 0.1):
  - 0/1 boundary: x0 = 4.61 at x1 = 0.1 down to 1.23 at x1 = 1.1. Slope about -3.3 (slightly
    steeper at the low end), and it leaves the box near x1 = 1.15.
  - 1/2 boundary: x0 = 6.69 at x1 = 0.1 down to 4.09 at x1 = 2.5. Slope about -1.1, with a little
    curvature of about ±0.03 in x0.
- I fitted each boundary with a line and snapped it to one decimal: `x0 + 3.3*x1 < 4.9` -> class 0,
  `x0 + 1.1*x1 > 6.8` -> class 2, otherwise class 1.
- Before snapping, I measured the checker's unsure zone (5% of the p95 top-2 margin, about 0.56).
  It is about ±0.08–0.1 wide in x0 around each boundary, so any line that stays within about 0.08
  of the true boundary passes. I checked candidate constants against the boundary table at
  x1 = 0.1, 0.7, 1.1 and 2.5 before running the checker.
- All 8 tanh units are gone. Result: PASS (snap), 0 confident disagreements. All disagreements are
  in the unsure zone, which is 4.3% of inputs. Exact is not reachable without keeping the tanh
  curvature.

#### Heuristics
- For a 2-input argmax net, first print the class map on a coarse grid. It shows at once how many
  boundaries there are and which classes touch.
- Bisect the boundary along one input for a sweep of values of the other input. A table of crossing
  points shows whether the boundary is straight; check the first differences.
- Compute the checker's unsure-zone width in input units (score margin / gradient, or measure it
  directly along a line). It tells you how much you can round coefficients. Pick the simplest
  1-decimal line that stays inside the zone at both ends and in the middle of the boundary.
- When two classes never touch, the argmax is a chain of one-sided tests, e.g. `if A: 0 elif B: 2
  else 1`. The order of the tests does not matter where their regions do not overlap.
- tanh nets with smooth boundaries usually reduce to linear rules for a snap pass. Mild curvature
  (well under the zone half-width) can be ignored.

</details>

```python
# 3-way classifier on two size-like inputs (petal_length in [1, 6.9], petal_width in [0.1, 2.5]).
# The tanh net's two decision boundaries are straight, parallel-ish lines in (petal_length, petal_width):
#   class 0 | class 1 : petal_length + 1.5 * petal_width = 3.5   (fitted: petal_length + 1.48 * petal_width = 3.51)
#   class 1 | class 2 : petal_length + 1.05 * petal_width = 6.7  (fitted: petal_length + 1.06 * petal_width = 6.72)
# Classes are ordered by overall size: small -> 0, medium -> 1, large -> 2.
def net(petal_length, petal_width):
    small_score = petal_length + 1.5 * petal_width
    large_score = petal_length + 1.05 * petal_width
    if small_score < 3.5:
        return 0
    if large_score < 6.7:
        return 1
    return 2
```

```python
    s2 = 0.79 * petal_length + petal_width - 4.02
    return argmax(0, s1, s2)
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What I did

- The dump is a 2 -> 8 (tanh) -> 3 argmax net. Instead of simplifying unit by unit, I mapped the
  decision regions on a grid (scratch/map.py). There are three bands ordered along a "size"
  direction: class 0 at small x0/x1, class 1 in the middle, class 2 at large values. Class 0 and
  class 2 never touch.
- I scanned rows of fixed x1 for class changes and fitted each boundary as x0 = a*x1 + b
  (scratch/bnd.py). Both are almost exactly straight (max residual about 0.001; the quadratic term is
  about 0.002):
  - 0|1: x0 + 1.48*x1 = 3.51
  - 1|2: x0 + 1.06*x1 = 6.72
- The program is two threshold tests on linear scores, applied in order (a decision list).
- Snapping: I tried several rounded versions and measured the largest net margin (top score minus
  second score) where program and net disagree (scratch/margin.py). The checker's unsure zone is 0.515.
  - 1.5 / 3.5 and 1.05 / 6.7: max 0.31. This is the version I chose, comfortably inside the zone.
  - x0 + x1 < 6.65 for the second boundary passes, but only barely (0.46); 6.6 and 6.68 fail.
    Too fragile.
  - x0 + x1 < 6.7 fails: the slope is really about 1.06, and over the x1 range of 2.4 a slope
    error of 0.06 shifts the line by about 0.15 at the edge.
- Result: `PASS (snap)`, with 0 disagreements where the net is confident. `PASS (exact)` is not
  reachable without keeping the tanh units, because the true boundaries are slightly curved.

#### Heuristics

- For low-dimensional argmax nets (2 inputs), map the class regions on a grid before touching the
  weights. Eight tanh units that combine into two straight boundaries are far easier to see from the
  output than from the units.
- Find boundary points by scanning one input at fixed values of the other for label changes, then
  fit x_a = slope*x_b + c. If the residual is tiny, a linear threshold is enough. Check a quadratic
  term to confirm there is no curvature.
- Ordered bands (0 | 1 | 2 along one direction, with 0 and 2 never touching) become a decision list:
  `if s1 < t1: 0; elif s2 < t2: 1; else 2`.
- Choose snaps by the largest net margin among disagreements, not just by pass/fail. A snap that
  passes close to the unsure-zone limit is fragile: new counterexamples or another seed would break it.
- A slope error matters more than an intercept error when the other input has a wide range. Snap the
  slope only if slope error × input range is small compared with the boundary's tolerance band.
- Express each boundary as x0 + k*x1 < c (coefficient of one input normalised to 1), so the snapped
  values stay readable.

</details>

```python
# 2 inputs -> 3 classes. The net draws two boundaries across the input box:
#   class 0 (low corner) below a gently curved line, class 2 beyond the diagonal petal_length + petal_width = 6.6,
#   class 1 in the band between them. Classes 0 and 2 never touch inside the input box.
def net(petal_length, petal_width):
    low_edge = 3.9 - 2.2 * petal_width + petal_width * petal_width / 3   # 0|1 boundary, as petal_length for a given petal_width
    if petal_length < low_edge:
        return 0
    if petal_length + petal_width > 6.6:
        return 2
    return 1
```

```python
    o2 = -0.96256554 * h1_0 - 3.0714757 * h1_1 + 5.56531 * h1_2 - 0.77084076 * h1_3 + 2.2376978 * h1_4 + 2.7367525152975603 * petal_length + 3.706290628845525 * petal_width - 19.481313813781497
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network does

The net is 2 inputs -> 8 tanh units -> 3 argmax scores. Its decision map over the input box
(x0 in [1, 6.9], x1 in [0.1, 2.5]) has three regions in diagonal bands:

- class 0 in the low corner (small x0 and small x1);
- class 1 in the middle band;
- class 2 in the high corner (large x0 + x1).

Classes 0 and 2 never touch inside the box, so the program is two tests in a row.

#### What I changed

1. **Looked at the decision map, not the units.** I printed the argmax class on a 60x25 grid. That
   showed two monotone boundaries, which makes the 8 tanh units unnecessary.
2. **Measured the boundaries.** For each x1 I root-solved where the pairwise score difference
   crosses 0. I also measured the width of the checker's unsure zone around each crossing (top-2
   margin < 5% of p95, about 0.485 here), which is how far a snapped boundary may move.
3. **1|2 boundary.** It is almost a line, x0 ~ 6.66 - 1.04*x1, with a slight bend at low x1. The
   unsure band is about +/-0.07 wide. `x0 + x1 > 6.6` stays inside the band at every x1, and so does
   the slope-1 diagonal. 6.55 and 6.5 fail, so 6.6 is the snapped constant.
4. **0|1 boundary.** It is clearly curved: the slope dx0/dx1 goes from about -1.8 at low x1 to
   about -1.05 at high x1. A single line does fail; I tried `x0 + 1.5*x1 < 3.7`, which gave 1631
   confident disagreements. A quadratic in x1 with simple coefficients,
   `x0 < 3.9 - 2.2*x1 + x1^2/3`, follows the measured crossings to within about 0.03, well inside
   the band.
5. Result: `PASS (snap)`, 0 confident disagreements. All differences are in the unsure zone, and
   most of them are along the 1|2 diagonal.

#### Heuristics

- For low-dimensional argmax nets, start from the decision map (class on a grid) rather than the
  hidden units. Many units are only there to bend one boundary slightly, and the readable program
  is a short if-chain over the boundaries.
- Find the order in which the regions are arranged (bands, corners) so that the if-chain can test
  one boundary at a time. Check that the non-adjacent classes really never meet inside the box.
- Measure each boundary by root-solving pairwise score differences (score_a - score_b = 0, with the
  third score below both) along one input. Also measure the checker's unsure-zone band around each
  crossing: that band is the snap budget for the boundary's position.
- Try a nice-coefficient line first (slope +/-1, simple fractions). If the boundary's slope changes
  by more than the band allows, express it as x0 = quadratic(x1) with simple coefficients rather
  than stacking line pieces. One product term is shorter than a min/max of 2-3 lines.
- Sweep the snapped constant a little (for example 6.5 / 6.55 / 6.6) and keep the simplest value
  that passes with 0 confident disagreements.
- Nets trained on clustered data extrapolate their boundaries smoothly into empty corners of the
  input box. The program has to follow the net there too, not the data's likely structure.

</details>

```python
def net(petal_length, petal_width):
    # Three classes separated by two straight cuts in the (petal_length, petal_width) box.
    # class 0: low corner (small petal_length and small petal_width)
    low_score = petal_length + 3.7 * petal_width
    if low_score < 5.1:
        return 0
    # class 2: high corner (roughly petal_length + petal_width large)
    high_score = petal_length + 1.1 * petal_width
    if high_score > 6.8:
        return 2
    # class 1: the band in between
    return 1
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network does
2 inputs (x0 in [1, 6.9], x1 in [0.1, 2.5]), 8 tanh hidden units, argmax over 3 classes.
Plotting the decision map on a grid showed three bands: class 0 in the low corner, class 2 in the
high corner, class 1 the wide band between. Class 0 and class 2 never touch inside the box.

#### What I changed
- Replaced all 8 tanh units and the 3 output scores with two linear tests.
- Located both boundaries by bisection along x0 for 25 values of x1, then least-squares fitted
  x0 as a line in x1:
  - 0/1 boundary: x0 = 5.136 - 3.700*x1 (max residual 0.001, essentially straight);
  - 1/2 boundary: x0 = 6.794 - 1.085*x1 (max residual 0.024, slightly curved).
- Snapped to `x0 + 3.7*x1 < 5.1 -> 0` and `x0 + 1.1*x1 > 6.8 -> 2`, else 1.
  Tested alternatives with check.py: the unsnapped fit (5.14 / 1.08 / 6.79) has fewer unsure-zone
  disagreements (3658 vs 5134) but both have 0 confident disagreements. Further snapping fails:
  `x0 + x1 > 6.75` (slope 1 instead of 1.1) gives 1326 confident disagreements, and the 0/1
  boundary does not tolerate slopes 3.5 or 4.
- Result: PASS (snap), 0 confident disagreements. Exact is not reachable with straight lines because
  the 1/2 boundary bends slightly.

#### Heuristics
- For 2-input argmax nets, first print a coarse ASCII class map over the input box; it shows how
  many regions there are, which pairs of classes share a boundary, and whether boundaries look straight.
- Find each pairwise boundary by bisection along one input for a sweep of the other, then fit a
  line; the max residual tells you whether a single linear test can work.
- Express each boundary as `x0 + a*x1 <op> t` (normalize one coefficient to 1), then snap `a` and `t`
  one at a time to round values, re-checking after each; keep the slope more precise than the
  threshold, since slope errors grow across the box while a threshold shift is uniform.
- With nested regions (class 0 | class 1 | class 2 in bands) an if-chain of half-plane tests
  (return the outer classes first, the middle class last) replaces the argmax completely.
- tanh hidden layers with many near-duplicate units (here pairs 1/2, 4/5, 6/7 with near-equal weights)
  usually implement just a few soft cuts; don't simplify unit by unit, read the decision geometry.

</details>

```python
def net(petal_length, petal_width):
    # Three ordered classes separated by two straight lines in the (petal_length, petal_width) plane.
    # class 0: small petal_length and petal_width (below the steep line petal_length + 2.4*petal_width = 4.2)
    # class 2: large petal_length and petal_width (above the diagonal petal_length + petal_width = 6.6)
    # class 1: the band in between
    size_low = petal_length + 2.4 * petal_width
    size_high = petal_length + petal_width
    if size_low < 4.2:
        return 0
    if size_high < 6.6:
        return 1
    return 2
```
