<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network does
A 2 -> 8 tanh -> 3 argmax classifier. Mapping argmax over a grid shows three ordered classes
in bands across the (x0, x1) plane, with no islands: class 0 at small x0/x1, class 2 at large
x0/x1, class 1 between them. Class 0 and class 2 never touch.

#### What I changed
- Dropped the 8 tanh units and the 3 score sums. Two linear decision boundaries replace them.
- Found the boundaries by scanning x0 at 49 fixed x1 values for argmax changes, then fitting
  `x0 = a*x1 + c` to the change points of each class pair:
  - 0|1: x0 = -2.405*x1 + 4.202 (max residual 0.006, so practically straight) -> snapped to
    `x0 + 2.4*x1 < 4.2`.
  - 1|2: x0 = -0.997*x1 + 6.588 (max residual 0.03, slightly curved) -> snapped to
    `x0 + x1 < 6.6`.
- Wrote the result as an ordered if-chain: test class 0 first, then 1, else 2.
- Result: `PASS (snap)`, with 0 disagreements where the net is confident and 3496 inside the unsure
  zone (mostly near the corner x0~6.5, x1~0.1, where the curved 1|2 boundary bends away from the line).
  An exact pass is not realistic with straight lines, because the tanh boundary is slightly curved.
- Tried other snaps: 6.5 fails (921 confident disagreements), 6.55 passes with more unsure
  disagreements (5148), and slope 2.5 / 4.25 fails (1 confident disagreement). So 2.4 / 4.2 / 1 / 6.6 is kept.

#### Heuristics
- For small argmax nets with 2 inputs, print the argmax over a coarse grid first. If the classes
  form ordered bands, the program is just a chain of linear threshold tests, and the hidden units
  do not need to be interpreted one by one.
- Locate boundaries by 1-D scans (fix one input, sweep the other on a fine grid, record class
  changes). Then fit `x_a = slope*x_b + c` for each class pair. The max residual tells you whether a
  straight line is enough.
- Snap slope and intercept to one decimal (or a simple fraction) and let the checker decide.
  When several snaps pass, keep the one with the fewest unsure-zone disagreements. When a boundary is
  curved, the fitted intercept is pulled by the curved end, so try the snapped value on both sides.
- When the classes are ordered bands, write the decision as an ordered if-chain (lowest class first).
  Each test then only needs the one boundary between adjacent classes, and pairs that never touch
  (here 0 and 2) need no test.
- Inputs bounded like x0 in [1, 6.9] and x1 in [0.1, 2.5], with three ordered classes, suggest a
  measurement-based classification. The boundaries have mixed slopes (one steep, one at 45 degrees),
  so keep the per-boundary weights rather than forcing one shared combination.

</details>

```python
# Two-feature, three-class classifier. The 8 tanh units only draw two nearly straight
# boundaries that cut the input box into three diagonal bands:
#   class 0: small petal_length and small petal_width   (petal_length + 2.8*petal_width < 4.5)
#   class 2: large petal_length and/or petal_width      (petal_length + petal_width >= 6.6)
#   class 1: the band in between
def net(petal_length, petal_width):
    low_score = petal_length + 2.8 * petal_width
    high_score = petal_length + petal_width
    if low_score < 4.5:
        return 0
    if high_score < 6.6:
        return 1
    return 2
```

```python
    s2 = 0.622198612522958 * petal_length + petal_width - 3.7094104974569952
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network does
2 float inputs (x0 in [1, 6.9], x1 in [0.1, 2.5]), 8 tanh hidden units, argmax over 3 classes.
Printing the predicted class on a 60x49 grid showed three bands separated by two almost straight
diagonal lines; the 8 hidden units are just a smooth way of drawing those two lines.

#### What I changed
- Located each class boundary by scanning x0 at fixed x1 and bisecting the class change,
  then least-squares fitted `x0 = a*x1 + b` to the boundary points.
  - 0|1 boundary: x0 = -2.80*x1 + 4.46 (max residual 0.005, essentially straight).
  - 1|2 boundary: x0 = -1.04*x1 + 6.68 (max residual 0.044, slightly curved).
- Replaced the whole net with two linear tests on the inputs:
  `x0 + 2.8*x1 < 4.5 -> 0`, else `x0 + x1 < 6.6 -> 1`, else `2`.
- Snapping: tried several rounded variants with check.py. Slope 2.8 could not be snapped to 3
  (1003 confident disagreements); slope 1.04 snapped to 1 with threshold 6.6 (6.65 failed,
  104 confident disagreements). Threshold 4.46 snapped to 4.5.
- Result: `PASS (snap)`, 0 disagreements where the net is confident; all differences are in the
  net's unsure zone along the boundaries. An exact pass is not realistic for a tanh net without
  keeping the tanh units themselves.

#### Heuristics
- For low-dimensional argmax nets, first print the class map on a grid; the number and shape of
  boundaries tell you the program structure (here: two lines -> an ordered if-chain).
- When classes appear as ordered bands, write the decision as a cascade of threshold tests
  (`if s1 < t1: 0 elif s2 < t2: 1 else 2`) instead of computing all scores and an argmax.
- Find boundaries numerically (scan + bisection along one input at fixed values of the other),
  then fit a line; the residual tells you whether a linear rule is enough.
- Normalize each boundary to coefficient 1 on one input (`x0 + a*x1 < b`) before snapping, so
  only one slope and one threshold need rounding.
- Snap greedily and verify each step: round slope to the nearest simple value, then re-tune the
  threshold; if a snap fails, step back to one decimal place. A slightly curved boundary can still
  snap to a straight line, with the threshold placed to balance the error at both ends.
- Smooth (tanh/sigmoid) hidden layers in small classifiers often just implement near-linear
  boundaries; do not try to simplify unit by unit when the end-to-end decision map is simple.

</details>

```python
def net(a, b, c):
    # The network computes the median of its three inputs.
    lo = min(a, b, c)
    hi = max(a, b, c)
    median = max(min(a, b), min(max(a, b), c))
    # Rounded corners: within 0.2 of a tie the net pulls the median toward the
    # tied neighbour's far side (by up to 0.075 at an exact tie).
    near_upper_tie = relu(0.2 - (hi - median))
    near_lower_tie = relu(0.2 - (median - lo))
    corner = 0.375 * (near_lower_tie - near_upper_tie)
    # Fit artefact: when the inputs span nearly the whole range (spread > 5),
    # the output bends up around median = -1 and down around median = +1.
    wide = relu(hi - lo - 5)
    bump_low = relu(1 - abs(median + 1))
    bump_high = relu(1 - abs(median - 1))
    wide_wave = wide * (0.12 * bump_low - 0.06 * bump_high)
    return median + corner + wide_wave
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network computes

The 3 -> 16 -> 16 -> 1 tanh network computes the **median of its three inputs**, with
fit noise of about ±0.16 on an output range of about 5.9.

How I found it:
- Symmetry probes. f(perm(x)) is close to f(x) for every permutation, with max deviation 0.13–0.18,
  which is fit noise. f(-x) is close to -f(x) (max deviation 0.09). So the function is symmetric and
  odd, with no dependence on input order.
- Correlation of the output with candidate features: median 0.9997, sum 0.89, min/max 0.58,
  |x_i| and pairwise products about 0. A least-squares fit y = a·median + b gives a = 1.0002 and
  b = 0.0025.

The median alone **fails** the check: 205 inputs beyond tolerance, max error 0.163, tolerance
0.1186. The leftover error has two systematic parts, and I modelled both.

1. **Rounded corners at ties.** The residual y − median, binned by the gap to the nearest
   neighbour, is the same at every median level:
   - upper tie (median ≈ max): −0.073 at gap 0, 0 at gap 0.2, +0.03 at gap 0.5, 0 at gap about 1.2;
   - lower tie: the mirror image.

   So the net rounds the corners of min/max with a smooth (tanh) kink, and the rounded min of the
   top two values undershoots. I kept only the main lobe: `0.375 * relu(0.2 - gap)`, which is 0.075
   at an exact tie and 0 beyond a gap of 0.2. This term alone cuts the check failures from 205 to 54.
2. **Wide-spread wave.** All remaining failures had lo ≈ −3 and hi ≈ +3, with spread > 5, which is
   the sparse corners of the cube. There the residual, as a function of the median, is about +0.07
   at median −1 and about −0.04 at median +1. I modelled it as `relu(spread − 5)` times two triangle
   bumps:
   - +0.12 at median −1;
   - −0.06 at median +1.

   I chose the coefficients with a minimax LP over about 3.3M points (uniform points, edge-biased
   points and the checker's set), then snapped them: 0.1225 → 0.12, −0.059 → −0.06, slope 0.375 = 3/8.
   This is a training artefact, not part of the task.

Result: `PASS (snap)`, max |program − net| = 0.103 against tolerance 0.1186, with 0 failures. It
passes the same way for checker seeds 1–3 (max 0.103–0.104) and for my own 3M-point uniform and
edge-biased sets (max 0.106). The bare median `max(min(x0,x1), min(max(x0,x1), x2))` is the intended
function. The other two terms only make the program follow the net's approximation error closely
enough to pass.

#### What I rejected

- Clipping the output to ±2.85 to model saturation at the cube corners. It had no effect once the
  tie term was in.
- A pull toward the mean, `c·(sum − 3·median)`. It made things worse for every c ≥ 0.02.
- Polynomial fits of the residual in (lo, med, hi). Degree 3 still fails, and higher degrees blow
  up. The residual that depends on permutation is as large as the symmetric part, so a symmetric
  smooth fit cannot capture it.
- Gated linear or quadratic terms in the raw inputs. They pass, but they are ugly and fitted to
  noise; the median-bump form is equally good with 2 coefficients.

#### Heuristics

- **Check symmetries first.** Compare f with f under each input permutation and each sign flip.
  Then correlate the output with order statistics (min, median, max), the sum, |x_i| and products.
  A correlation of 0.9997 with the median was decisive at once.
- **Order statistics as a basis.** When a function is symmetric under permutation, rewrite it in
  sorted coordinates (lo, med, hi) and the gaps (med − lo, hi − med). Residuals then become 1-D
  curves.
- **Tanh/sigmoid nets that approximate min/max/median/abs have rounded kinks.** Near a tie the
  output is offset by a fixed amount (here 0.075, about 1.3% of the range), and the offset dies out
  within a small gap (here 0.2). It does not depend on location. Bin the residual by the gap to see
  it, and model it with `k*relu(w - gap)`, which is linear in the language.
- **Wide-spread corners are poorly trained.** Points where the inputs sit at opposite extremes of
  the cube are rare under uniform training data, so the biggest errors cluster there. Gate any
  correction with `relu(spread - w)` so that it cannot disturb the rest of the domain.
- **Fit corrections by minimax, then snap.** The snap criterion is a maximum error, not RMS. A
  cutting-plane LP (`scipy.optimize.linprog` on a working set that grows with the worst violators)
  finds a sparse correction with the fewest terms. Least squares left failures where minimax
  passed.
- **Test on more than the checker's set.** Use an edge-biased sample (most coordinates within 10%
  of a bound) and other checker seeds, so that the margin is real and not luck of one sample.
- **Compute in NumPy.** The checker's language evaluator is slow on millions of points. Cache the
  checker's input set (`build_input_set`) and evaluate candidate formulas in NumPy, and use
  `check.py` only to confirm.

</details>

```python
def net(a, b, c):
    # The network computes the median of its three inputs.
    lo = min(a, b, c)
    hi = max(a, b, c)
    median = a + b + c - lo - hi
    # tanh units round the corners where the median ties with a neighbour:
    # a near-tie with the smallest input pulls the output up, a near-tie with
    # the largest pulls it down (a quadratic soft-min / soft-max of width 0.4).
    tie_low = relu(0.4 - (median - lo))
    tie_high = relu(0.4 - (hi - median))
    corner = 0.4 * (tie_low * tie_low - tie_high * tie_high)
    # when the inputs span almost the whole box (spread > 5 of 6) the net
    # saturates and shrinks the median slightly toward 0
    shrink = median * relu(hi - lo - 5) / 60
    return median + corner - shrink - 0.01
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes: 3 inputs -> 16 -> 16 -> 1 tanh regression net

#### What the net computes
The **median of x0, x1, x2**. Slices made this obvious: with two inputs fixed at -2 and 2, the
output followed the third input exactly between them and flattened to -2 / 2 outside. A least-squares
fit of the net against `median(x)` over 200k uniform points gave slope 0.997, intercept -0.009, RMS
error 0.032.

The median is written branch-free as `x0 + x1 + x2 - min(...) - max(...)`.

#### Why pure median is not enough
`median` alone fails the snap check (max error 0.24 vs tolerance 0.117). The residual has two
systematic parts, both smoothing artefacts of tanh units:

1. **Rounded corners at ties.** Where the median is close to the largest input the net acts as a soft
   min and comes out low (about -0.14 at an exact tie); where it is close to the smallest input the
   net comes out high. Binning the residual by the gap to each neighbour showed a bump of width about
   0.3–0.4. Modelled as `0.4 * (relu(0.4 - gap_low)^2 - relu(0.4 - gap_high)^2)` (fitted coefficient
   0.395, width 0.4).
2. **Saturation at the box extremes.** When lo is near -3 and hi near +3 the net shrinks the median
   toward 0 by up to ~5%. Regression on candidate features (`med*spread`, `med^3`, `med*relu(spread-s)`)
   showed only `median * relu(spread - 5)` mattered (fitted 1/60). A constant offset of -0.01 (the
   mean residual) completes it.

Result: `PASS (snap)`, max error 0.112 against tolerance 0.117. Variations (no offset, no shrink,
width 0.3 or 0.5, shrink 1/40 or threshold 4.5) all failed, so all three corrections are needed for
this particular net. The core semantics is just the median; the rest describes the net's error, not
the task.

#### Heuristics
- Before reading weights, probe a small grid of slices (fix all inputs but one at a few values,
  sweep the last). Order-statistic functions (min, max, median, clamp) show up as piecewise-linear
  slices with slope 0 or 1 whose breakpoints equal the other inputs' values.
- Fit `y` against the candidate formula (slope/intercept/RMS) before refining; slope ~1 and
  intercept ~0 confirm the hypothesis.
- Median of three in a min/max language: `a + b + c - min(a,b,c) - max(a,b,c)`.
- Smooth-activation (tanh/sigmoid) nets that approximate a piecewise-linear function deviate most at
  its kinks. Bin the residual by the distance to each kink; a soft-min/soft-max correction
  `c * relu(w - gap)^2` (signed by which side the kink bends) usually captures it.
- Second, check residuals against "how close to the input box corners" features (spread, |x| near
  the bound): tanh nets often saturate and shrink the output at the extremes of the training domain.
  A hinge on spread (`relu(spread - s)`) localises the fix to the corners.
- Use least squares to pick candidate features, then tune the few snapped constants against the
  checker's max error, since acceptance is a max-error criterion, not RMS.
- Add the mean residual as a constant offset when the tolerance margin is tight.

</details>

```python
def net(a, b, c):
    # The network computes the median of its three inputs.
    lo = min(a, b, c)
    hi = max(a, b, c)
    mid = a + b + c - lo - hi
    # Distance from the median to the smallest and to the largest input.
    gap_lo = mid - lo
    gap_hi = hi - mid
    # A smooth correction that depends only on the two gaps. It reproduces how the network
    # deviates from the exact median: near a tie the median is pulled toward the odd
    # input out (the network rounds off the kink), and there is a slight pull toward
    # the mean. Plain `mid` is off by up to 0.22, against a snap tolerance of 0.119.
    t0 = tanh(0.655 * gap_lo - 0.338 * gap_hi + 0.787)
    t1 = tanh(-0.091 * gap_lo + 0.114 * gap_hi - 0.203)
    t2 = tanh(-0.262 * gap_lo + 0.822 * gap_hi + 0.248)
    t3 = tanh(2.156 * gap_lo - 0.051 * gap_hi + 0.668)
    t4 = tanh(0.05 * gap_lo - 1.599 * gap_hi - 0.786)
    return mid + 0.298 * t0 + 0.866 * t1 - 0.27 * t2 - 0.335 * t3 - 0.504 * t4 - 0.068
```


<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network computes

The net is a 3 → 16 → 16 → 1 tanh regression net on x0, x1, x2 ∈ [-3, 3]. It approximates the
**median of the three inputs**.

- A least-squares fit to linear and quadratic terms gives about 0.4·(x0+x1+x2), with error up to 1.6.
- Candidate symmetric functions (max, min, sum, median, max|x|) were compared against the net. The
  median has correlation 0.9997, slope 1.003 and bias 0.007.
- Plain `mid = x0 + x1 + x2 - min - max` FAILs the check: max error 0.25, against a snap tolerance
  of 0.119 (output range about 6). About 0.3% of points are outside the tolerance.

#### Where the net departs from the exact median

- **Ties.** Where two inputs nearly tie, the median has a kink. The net rounds the kink off, which
  pulls the output about 0.1 toward the third input. For example, at x0 = x1 = t with x2 = -3 the net
  gives about t - 0.1.
- **Corners.** The pull grows to about 0.25 at the corners, such as (3, 3, -3) → 2.75.
- **Mean.** There is a slight overall pull toward the mean.
- **Asymmetry.** The net is not exactly symmetric under permuting the inputs: outputs over the 6
  permutations of one point spread by up to 0.19. It is not exactly odd either: |f(x) + f(-x)| is
  up to 0.07.

#### What I did

1. Wrote the program as `lo = min`, `hi = max`, `mid = sum - lo - hi`, with
   `gap_lo = mid - lo` and `gap_hi = hi - mid`.
2. Fitted the residual net - median with a few tanh units. To fit for the worst case, I used Adam
   with an Lp loss and stepped p up through 2, 6 and 12. Training points were uniform samples plus
   extra samples on the faces and corners of the box and near ties.
3. Compared feature sets for the correction:
   - raw x only: max error 0.18–0.22 with 2–4 units;
   - raw x plus sorted values: 0.10 with 6 units;
   - (gap_lo, gap_hi, mid): 0.099 with 5 units;
   - (gap_lo, gap_hi) only: 0.10 with 5 units. The fitted weights on `mid` went to about 0, so the
     correction depends only on the two gaps, which makes it a function of the gaps alone (shift
     equivariant: adding c to every input adds c to the output).
   - Separable models f(gap_lo) - g(gap_hi) plus a skew term (gap_hi - gap_lo) plateau at about
     0.113. The gaps interact.
   - An odd-symmetric correction plateaus at about 0.15: the net's asymmetry is too large.
4. Final program: median plus 5 tanh units in (gap_lo, gap_hi). Coefficients are rounded to 3
   decimals. At 2 decimals the max error reached 0.1204, just over the 0.119 tolerance, because units
   t1 and t2 partly cancel.

Result: `PASS (snap)`, max |program - net| = 0.0949 against tolerance 0.119. On 5M extra
uniform, face and grid points (my own stress set) the worst error is 0.103.

`PASS (exact)` is not realistic short of re-emitting the net: its departures from the median are
asymmetric, unit-scale "noise" from training.

#### Heuristics

- **Test order statistics early.** For a regression net on a few inputs with equal ranges, compare
  against max, min, median and sum (and their abs versions) before fitting polynomials. A
  correlation near 1 with slope about 1 identifies the core function at once. The median can be
  written in the program language as `x0 + x1 + x2 - min(...) - max(...)`.
- **Look at where the residual is large, not just how large.** Bin the residual by the gaps between
  sorted inputs and by position in the box. Smooth nets (tanh/sigmoid) round off every kink of a
  piecewise-linear target. Near a tie of two order statistics the output is pulled about w/4 toward
  the odd input out, where w is the width of the smoothing. Corners of the input box are where these
  errors are largest.
- **Use the right coordinates for the correction.** For order-statistic targets, use the sorted
  values and the gaps between them. Use (gap_lo, gap_hi) rather than raw inputs: a tanh correction
  in raw x needed more units for a worse fit. If fitted weights on a feature collapse to 0, drop it.
  This gives an invariance (here, shift equivariance).
- **Fit for the worst case.** The check is max-error based. Fit residual units with a soft-minimax
  loss (an Lp loss, raising p over training). Sample extra points on the faces and corners of the
  box and near ties, because that is where the checker's worst points are.
- **Check symmetry before imposing it.** Measure the permutation spread and |f(x) + f(-x)| of the net
  first. If either is a sizeable share of the tolerance, a symmetric or odd correction will plateau
  above the tolerance.
- **Round coefficients last, and check every rounding.** Units that partly cancel, such as two tanh
  units with similar arguments and opposite output weights, are sensitive to rounding. Keep enough
  digits for those.
- **Report the plain formula alongside the correction.** When the clean formula fails only by the
  net's own smoothing error, say so. The correction terms describe the net's imperfections, not the
  task.

</details>

```python
# dumped from a neural network: 2 inputs -> 8 -> 1 outputs

    h1_1 = tanh(-0.097558 * a - 0.09474709 * b + 2.2248003)  # layer 1 neuron 1
    h1_2 = tanh(-0.06102251 * a - 0.0025333497 * b - 1.8193926)  # layer 1 neuron 2
    h1_3 = tanh(-6.245161 * a + 6.261762 * b - 1.2560972)  # layer 1 neuron 3
    h1_4 = tanh(0.041964587 * a - 0.012702305 * b + 1.7491381)  # layer 1 neuron 4
    h1_5 = tanh(-0.09287194 * a - 0.092859425 * b + 2.1484022)  # layer 1 neuron 5
    h1_6 = tanh(0.01703467 * a - 0.023337938 * b + 1.7678183)  # layer 1 neuron 6
    h1_7 = tanh(-6.3650265 * a + 6.3797417 * b + 1.2639687)  # layer 1 neuron 7
    return -1.7769015 * h1_0 - 1.7839073 * h1_1 + 1.824469 * h1_2 - 11.852298 * h1_3 - 1.7747521 * h1_4 - 1.8475012 * h1_5 - 1.4369618 * h1_6 + 11.670899 * h1_7 - 1.4596305
```

```python
# Binary classifier: class 1 when the two inputs are within 0.2 of each other.
def net(a, b):
    gap = abs(b - a)
    return 0.2 - gap
```

AST tool (no LLM, `ast4`):

```python
# produced by nndc ast (route: kink-merge)
def net(a, b):
    return 0.85 * a - 0.86 * b - 9.75 - 101 * relu(a - b) + 49.5 * relu(a - b + 0.4) + 50.5 * relu(a - b - 0.4)
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Agent notes

#### What the network does
Two units carry the decision: h1_3 = tanh(6.25*(x1 - x0) - 1.256) and h1_7 = tanh(6.37*(x1 - x0) + 1.264).
Both have input weights of nearly equal size and opposite sign on x0 and x1, so they depend only on
d = x1 - x0. Their thresholds are d = +0.20 and d = -0.20, and the output weights (-11.85, +11.67) are
nearly equal and opposite, so together they form a bump: high when -0.2 < d < 0.2, low outside.

The other six units have tiny slopes (|w| <= 0.11) and large biases. On [-2, 2]^2 they stay near
tanh(+-1.8..2.2), roughly +-0.95, so they act as constants. With the output bias they sum to about
-11.5. That offset moves the output from ~+8.5 inside the band to ~-11.5 outside.

A boundary scan along d for x0 in {-2..2} put the crossings at |d| from 0.199 to 0.207. The spread comes
from the small slopes of the "constant" units. Snapped threshold: 0.2.

#### Result
`return 0.2 - abs(x1 - x0)`: class 1 iff |x1 - x0| < 0.2 (a "nearly equal" test).
check.py: PASS (snap), 0 disagreements where the net is confident. All 9349 disagreements are in
the unsure zone along the band edges.

#### Heuristics
- Find units whose weights on two inputs are nearly equal and opposite (w, -w). They depend on the
  difference of those inputs, so rewrite them in terms of d = xi - xj.
- Units with small weights relative to their bias (|w|*range << |b|) are constants on the input box.
  Fold them, with the output bias, into one offset.
- A pair of steep sigmoid/tanh units on the same direction, with output weights of opposite sign and
  thresholds at -t and +t, is a window/bump: a test of the form |d| < t. The offset decides whether
  the inside or the outside of the window is class 1.
- To find thresholds, scan the real net along the found direction at several offsets and snap the
  average crossing to a simple value (0.2 here). Residual drift of a few thousandths along the band
  usually comes from the near-constant units and is safe to drop for a snap pass.
- For binary outputs, returning a signed margin (t - |d|) keeps the same decision and is easy to read.

</details>

```python
# inputs: a: float [-2, 2], b: float [-2, 2]
# output: binary: class 1 if the returned value > 0, else class 0
def net(a, b):
    h1_0 = tanh(0.1240633 * a - 0.04543848 * b + 1.8683441)  # layer 1 neuron 0
    h1_1 = tanh(0.10080172 * a - 0.068331376 * b + 1.9149671)  # layer 1 neuron 1
    h1_2 = tanh(-6.8880982 * a + 6.884908 * b + 1.3238795)  # layer 1 neuron 2
    h1_3 = tanh(-6.2642107 * a + 6.254124 * b - 1.2753283)  # layer 1 neuron 3
    h1_4 = tanh(-0.015286752 * a - 0.049781978 * b + 0.18500075)  # layer 1 neuron 4
    h1_5 = tanh(0.0545902 * a + 0.1551242 * b - 1.8997715)  # layer 1 neuron 5
    h1_6 = tanh(-0.04336431 * a + 0.100643754 * b - 1.8219378)  # layer 1 neuron 6
    h1_7 = tanh(-0.1383918 * a + 0.041576173 * b - 1.649837)  # layer 1 neuron 7
    return -2.3291981 * h1_0 - 2.1461663 * h1_1 + 10.92958 * h1_2 - 11.7136 * h1_3 - 0.10093871 * h1_4 + 1.998914 * h1_5 + 2.1911304 * h1_6 + 2.0427074 * h1_7 - 0.9051256
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### Result

```python
def net(x0, x1):
    return abs(x1 - x0) < 0.2
```

`check.py`: PASS (snap), 0 disagreements where the net is confident; 5121 disagreements (0.77% of
inputs), all in the unsure zone along the two boundary lines.

The net says class 1 when the two inputs are within 0.2 of each other ("near equal").

#### What I changed and why

- **Two steep units carry the decision.** `h1_2 = tanh(-6.888 x0 + 6.885 x1 + 1.324)` and
  `h1_3 = tanh(-6.264 x0 + 6.254 x1 - 1.275)` both have weights of about +-k on (x0, x1), so they
  are steps in `d = x1 - x0`. Their midpoints are at d = -1.324/6.886 = -0.192 and
  d = 1.275/6.259 = +0.204. Output weights +10.93 and -11.71 with opposite signs make a
  "window": high between the two steps, low outside.
- **Six units are nearly constant.** h1_0, h1_1, h1_4..h1_7 have input weights <= 0.16 and
  biases around +-1.9 (or 0.185 with a 0.10 output weight), so over [-2, 2]^2 they barely move.
  Together with the output bias they sum to about -11.06 (a linear fit gives
  -11.06 - 0.084 x0 + 0.090 x1, max error 0.09). I folded them into a constant.
- **Sign table.** With that constant: d << -0.2 gives -10.93 + 11.71 - 11.06 < 0; inside the
  window +10.93 + 11.71 - 11.06 > 0; d >> 0.2 gives +10.93 - 11.71 - 11.06 < 0. So the
  decision is `-0.2 < d < 0.2`.
- **Exact boundaries.** Solving net = 0 numerically along d for x0 in [-2, 2] gives lower
  boundaries -0.1981..-0.1997 and upper 0.1978..0.2049, so both snap to 0.2 (shared threshold,
  symmetric), written as `abs(x1 - x0) < 0.2`. The remaining drift (up to 0.005 at x0 = 2)
  comes from the slightly unequal weights on x0 and x1 in the steep units and the small linear
  tilt of the background units, and lies inside the net's unsure zone.
- I did not try for PASS (exact): the folded background is off by up to 0.09 in score, which
  moves the boundary by about 0.001, so an exact version would have to keep all 8 tanh units.

#### Heuristics

- **Split units by steepness.** In a tanh/sigmoid layer, units with |w| * input range much less
  than 1 and |bias| > ~1.5 are saturated or near-linear over the domain. Fold them (with the
  output bias) into a constant, or a linear term if needed. The few steep units carry the logic.
- **Equal-and-opposite input weights mean a difference variable.** A unit with weights (-k, +k)
  depends only on `x1 - x0`; its step sits at `d = -bias / k`.
- **Two steps of opposite output sign on the same variable form a window.** A positive weight on
  an increasing step at d = a and a negative weight on an increasing step at d = b > a give
  "high when a < d < b". When a = -b, write it as `abs(d) < b`.
- **Evaluate the sign table with the folded constant** to decide which regions are class 1,
  rather than reading weight signs alone.
- **Measure the real boundary numerically** (root-find the net along the relevant direction at
  several positions) before snapping. The step midpoint `-bias/k` is only approximate (here
  -0.192/0.204) because the other terms shift the zero crossing; the actual roots were much
  closer to +-0.2.
- **Grid ties.** Checkers that sample on grids hit points exactly on a snapped boundary
  (here d = 0.2). Disagreements there are expected and fall in the unsure zone.

</details>

```python
def net(a, b):
    return -0.4779791730896406 * a + 0.631127620852611 * b - 15.776151838566543 - 104.56470610822215 * relu(a - 0.9986091739720095 * b + 0.009013433347861767) + 56.23293533813904 * relu(0.9955515283585897 * a - b + 0.4785404620435436) + 49.29963529555376 * relu(0.99797967677375 * a - b - 0.40459036793581904)
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network computes

Class 1 iff `|x1 - x0| < 0.2`: the two inputs are nearly equal. `agent.py` returns `0.2 - abs(x1 - x0)`.
It passes `check.py` as `PASS (snap)`: 0 disagreements where the net is confident, and 5784 inside the
net's unsure zone (about 1% of inputs, all within about 0.01 of the decision line).

#### How I got there

- Grouped the 8 tanh units by weight size:
  - Units 4, 5 and 6 have large, nearly opposite weights on x0 and x1 (about ±6.76, ±3.67 and ±5.76).
    Each is a soft step in `d = x1 - x0`, with switch points at d = -0.19, +0.26 and +0.22.
    Their output weights (-11.7, -4.5, -10.5) together make a bump: about +22 near d = 0 and about ±3 far away.
  - Units 0, 1, 2, 3 and 7 have small input weights (< 0.31) and biases around ±2.1, so they stay near
    saturation (tanh ≈ ±0.97) over the whole domain. Together they act as a nearly constant offset
    with a slight tilt, which pushes the far tails below 0.
- Scanned the decision boundary along lines with fixed x0. The crossings sit at d ≈ -0.207..-0.200
  and d ≈ +0.199..+0.211, drifting slightly (about 0.004 per unit of x0) because of the slow units.
  That drift is noise, so I snapped both edges to ±0.2, giving one shared threshold on |d|.
- `PASS (exact)` would require reproducing the net's slightly tilted, slightly curved boundary to
  float precision, which means keeping essentially all 8 units. I did not keep them, because the
  snapped formula is the meaningful one.

#### Heuristics

- For tanh/sigmoid nets, sort units by input-weight size. Units with small input weights and large
  |bias| saturate across the domain, so treat them as constants plus a small tilt, not as features.
- When a unit's input weights are nearly equal and opposite (w, -w), it is a function of the
  difference x1 - x0. Several such units with switch points on both sides of 0 and opposite-signed output
  weights form a band or window detector: `|x1 - x0| < c`.
- For binary outputs, scan the sign change of the dump along a few 1-D slices. The crossings give the
  thresholds directly. Then snap them to a round shared value (here ±0.2) and write the score as
  `c - abs(...)`, so the sign carries the decision.
- Expect a few disagreements in the unsure zone after snapping. They are fine for snap, but exact needs the full net.

</details>

```python
    h1_6 = tanh(-0.2718507 * income - 3.7730043)  # layer 1 neuron 6
    h1_7 = tanh(0.043727506 * income - 6.3360105)  # layer 1 neuron 7
    return 6.9177465 * h1_0 + 6.988588 * h1_1 - 7.008162 * h1_2 - 7.0372314 * h1_3 - 6.675667 * h1_4 + 7.5225267 * h1_5 - 6.8768086 * h1_6 + 7.3729334 * h1_7 + 6.746825
```

```python
def net(income):
    # Convex, piecewise-linear "bracket" curve: marginal rate 0.15 up to 50,
    # 0.25 from 50 to 90, 0.40 above 90 (continuous at both breakpoints).
    rate_low = 0.15 * income
    rate_mid = 0.25 * income - 5
    rate_high = 0.4 * income - 18.5
    return max(rate_low, rate_mid, rate_high)
```


<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network is
1 input x0 in [0, 200] -> 8 tanh units -> 1 regression output. Output runs from about -0.45 (x0=0)
to about 60.9 (x0=200), and it rises the whole way.

#### What I changed and why
- **Unit census.** I computed each unit's centre (-b/a), width (1/a) and output weight.
  - Units 0, 4 and 6 have centres outside [0, 200] (-272, -11.7, -13.9). Over the domain they are
    almost constant; units 4 and 6 are saturated at -1.
  - Units 2 and 3 have centres at -6.6 and 105 with wide transitions, so they act mostly as
    near-linear ramps.
  - Units 1, 5 and 7 (centres 68, 181, 145) shape the rest.
  - All |output weights| are about 7. This is the usual "sum of many saturating tanh pieces
    approximating a ramp" pattern. The individual units carry no meaning.
- **Read off the slope profile.** The numerical derivative is about 0.15 on [0, 40]. It rises
  smoothly to about 0.40 around x0 = 95–100, then wobbles between 0.38 and 0.44 up to 185.
  Near 200 it drops (an edge artifact of unit 5's tanh centred at 181). So the function is convex
  and piecewise-linear-like: a low marginal rate, then a higher one.
- **Fit with nice coefficients.** I ran grid searches over 2- and 3-piece continuous
  piecewise-linear fits, using slopes in 0.05/0.01 steps and knots in steps of 5/10.
  - The best unconstrained minimax 3-piece fit had error 0.67. Its parameters: intercept 0,
    knots 58 and 88, slopes 0.156, 0.295 and 0.391. The remaining error comes from the net's own
    wiggles.
  - The best 2-piece fit had error 0.77 (knot 75, slopes 0.18 and 0.39, intercept -0.75), which
    is less clean.
  - I chose slopes 0.15 / 0.25 / 0.40 with knots 50 and 90 and intercept 0, continuous at both
    knots. As a max of lines it is `max(0.15x, 0.25x - 5, 0.4x - 18.5)`.
- **Result:** `PASS (snap)`. The max error is 0.997 against a tolerance of 1.228, about 1.6% of
  the output range. The worst point is x0 ≈ 117, inside the net's slope wobble.
- **Exact is not reachable** with a simpler program. Even dropping the saturated unit 4 changes
  the output by up to 6e-4 near x0 = 0, which is more than atol 1e-5 + rtol 1e-4·|y| there. Any
  exact version would be the dump itself, so I chose the readable snapped formula.

#### Heuristics
- For 1-input tanh/sigmoid nets, start with a unit census: centre = -b/a, width = 1/|a|, and the
  output weight. Units whose centre lies more than about 3 widths outside the domain are
  constants (or linear terms, if the width is large). Large, equal-magnitude output weights (all
  about ±7 here) mean the units are tiling a ramp, not encoding separate features.
- Look at the numerical derivative of the whole net on a fine grid before looking at units. A
  plateau-valued derivative profile (0.15, then 0.4) points to a piecewise-linear target. Wobble
  around a plateau is fitting noise and should not become extra pieces.
- Ignore derivative changes within about one unit width of the domain edge. They usually come
  from a tanh centred near the edge (here a drop near x=200 from the unit centred at 181).
- Convex piecewise-linear functions read best as `max(line1, line2, line3)` (and concave ones
  as `min`), with slopes snapped first and intercepts chosen so the pieces stay continuous at
  round knots.
- Do a free minimax fit first to see the error floor (0.67 here). Then snap slopes, knots and
  intercept to round values and accept the snap if the error stays well under the tolerance
  (here 0.997 against 1.228).
- A tanh net is almost never `PASS (exact)` after removing units. Saturated units still differ
  from ±1 by about 1e-4 near the domain edge, which exceeds the exact tolerance at small |y|.
  Go straight for a meaningful snapped formula.

</details>

```python
def net(income):
    return max(0.19 * income - 1, 0.39 * income - 16.8)
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network is

1 input `x0` in [0, 200], 8 tanh units, regression output. Every output weight is about +-7, so
each unit is a smooth step of height about 14.

Normalising each unit to `w * tanh(k * (x0 - c))` with `k > 0` (using `tanh(-z) = -tanh(z)`) shows
that all units rise, and gives their centres:

| unit | centre c | slope k | on [0, 200] |
|------|----------|---------|-------------|
| 6 | 0.19 | 0.018 | soft rising step |
| 0 | 72.3 | 0.024 | soft rising step |
| 1 | 108.3 | 0.043 | soft rising step |
| 3 | 149.0 | 0.042 | soft rising step |
| 7 | 183.6 | 0.049 | soft rising step |
| 2 | -31.2 | 0.117 | saturated at +1 (tanh >= 0.997) |
| 4 | -1.67 | 2.31 | saturated (tanh <= -0.999) |
| 5 | -8.2 | 0.48 | saturated at +1 (tanh >= 0.999) |

Steps 1: fold the three saturated units into the constant (27.65). This 5-tanh version
(`scratch/tanh5.py`) passes snap with max error 0.02.

Step 2: the steps are wide (widths 40-100) and overlap, so their sum is close to a ramp.
The numerical derivative is about 0.16-0.19 for x0 < 70 and about 0.39-0.41 for x0 > 100
(with a drop near 200 from the last step's edge). A least-squares fit with one hinge put the
knot at 81. A grid search over rounded coefficients gave

    net(x0) = max(0.19*x0 - 1, 0.39*x0 - 17) = 0.19*x0 - 1 + 0.2*relu(x0 - 80)

with max |program - net| = 0.64 (tolerance 1.22, which is 2% of the output range of 61). Final
`agent.py` is this one-hinge formula. It passes at the snap level, not exact: 8 tanh units became
one hinge. Rounder variants did worse: `max(0.16x, 0.4x-20)` had error 2.4,
`0.2x - 1 + 0.2 relu(x-80)` had 2.4, and `max(0.2x-1.5, 0.4x-19)` had 1.09, which is too close to
the tolerance.

Interpretation: the output rises at about 0.19 per unit up to x0 = 80, then at about 0.39 per
unit. It looks like a two-bracket rate schedule.

#### Heuristics

- Normalise every tanh/sigmoid unit to `w * act(k*(x - c))` with `k > 0`, using
  `tanh(-z) = -tanh(z)`. Then the centre `c = -b/a` and the sign of `w` show which way each
  step goes. Read it as a table of steps.
- A unit whose centre is outside the input box by more than about 3/k is saturated. Fold it into
  the output constant (`w * tanh(+-inf) = +-w`). Check how far from +-1 it is at the nearest edge.
- When every output weight has about the same size (here +-7), the net is building a function
  from equal-height steps. If the step widths (about 2/k) are larger than the gaps between
  centres, the sum is a smooth ramp, not a staircase. Look at the numerical derivative and fit
  piecewise-linear pieces, not thresholds.
- For 1-input regression: tabulate the net, take the gradient, find the slope plateaus, fit
  hinges by least squares (`[1, x, relu(x-k)...]`), then grid-search rounded coefficients and
  keep the simplest that stays well under the tolerance (aim for about half of it, because the
  checker samples densely, including boundary points).
- Edge effects: a step centred near the domain edge bends the curve there (here the slope drops
  at x0 = 200). A hinge fit should tolerate this rather than add a piece for it.
- Keep a faithful intermediate (saturated units removed, exact rewrites) and check it first, to
  separate "my algebra is right" from "my approximation is good enough".

</details>

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Agent notes

#### What the network does

One float input x0 in [0, 200], regression output from 8 tanh units. Reading each unit as a
smooth step `tanh(w*(x0 - c))` with centre `c = -b/w`:

- three units (h1_3, h1_4, h1_7) have centres far outside the domain (-297, -14, -387), so they
  are almost constant over [0, 200] (total variation about 0.1) and only shift the bias;
- the other five are steps of height about +-7 with centres near 0, 73, 109, 149 and 184
  (h1_1 and h1_6 have negative input weight and negative output weight, so they also rise).

Five rising steps of similar height with growing density give a monotone, convex-then-straight
curve: about -0.5 at x0 = 0, slope ~0.16 up to x0 ~ 70, slope ~0.39 from ~100 to ~190, and a
small flattening at the right edge (the last step is centred at 184 and is still unfinished at 200).

#### What I changed

Replaced the whole net with a two-piece convex linear function:

    max(0.17 * x0, 0.39 * x0 - 17)      # knee at x0 ~ 77

- I tabulated the net, then fit piecewise-linear models with one and two knots (least squares)
  to see how many pieces are needed. One knot already gave max error ~0.96 against a snap
  tolerance of 1.22 (2% of the output range ~61).
- Then I grid-searched rounded coefficients for `max(a*x0 + b, c*x0 + d)` by max error.
  Slope 0.4 fails (error 1.46 at x0 = 200, where the net bends down); 0.39 with intercept -17
  and a zero-intercept first piece fits with max error 0.92.
- Result: `PASS (snap)`, max |program - net| = 0.923 (worst near x0 = 35, where the net is
  slightly convex and the program is a straight line). `PASS (exact)` is not reachable without
  keeping all the tanh units, since dropping even the nearly constant ones moves the output by
  ~0.1, far above the exact tolerance.

#### Heuristics

- For a tanh/sigmoid unit, compute its centre `-b/w` and its width `1/|w|`. Units whose centre
  lies more than ~3 widths outside the input domain are constants on the domain; fold them into
  the bias.
- A unit with negative input weight and negative output weight is a rising step; normalise signs
  before reading the shape (tanh is odd).
- Several same-height steps spread across the domain add up to a smooth ramp; their spacing
  gives the local slope (step height / gap). Denser steps mean a steeper slope, so a curve made
  of unevenly spaced equal steps is a piecewise-linear function in disguise.
- For 1-input regression, tabulate the net and do a least-squares piecewise-linear fit with 1 and
  2 knots first: it shows quickly how many pieces the snap tolerance needs.
- A convex 2-piece fit is `max(line1, line2)`; this is shorter and clearer than `relu` sums.
- When rounding slopes, check the domain ends: the last step is often only half-finished at the
  boundary, so the net flattens there and a "nicer" slope (0.4 vs 0.39) can fail at the edge.
- Grid-search snapped coefficients by max (not mean) error, since the checker uses max error.

</details>

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network does
1 float input in [-2, 2], binary output, 4 tanh hidden units.

- `h1_0` and `h1_3` have near-zero input weights (|w| < 0.002) and large biases, so they are
  constants over the domain (tanh(-2.54) ~ -0.988, tanh(2.80) ~ 0.993). Folded into the bias.
- `h1_1 = tanh(8.13*(x0 - 0.49995))` is a soft step up at x0 ~ +0.5.
- `h1_2 = tanh(-8.31*(x0 + 0.49844))` is a soft step down at x0 ~ -0.5.
- Output = -8.1*h1_1 - 8.1*h1_2 + const(~ -7.94). The two steps with equal negative weights form
  a bump: score ~ +8.24 in the middle, ~ -7.94 on both sides.

Numerically scanning the score on a 4M-point grid gives zero crossings at x0 = -0.500792 and
x0 = 0.502291. So: class 1 iff x0 is inside a window of half-width 1/2 around 0.

#### Final program
`return abs(x0) < 0.5` - PASS (snap); all disagreements are in the net's unsure zone right at the
edges. A version with the raw thresholds (`-0.500792 < x0 < 0.502291`) passes exact, but the
snapped symmetric window is the meaningful one, so `agent.py` keeps it.

#### Heuristics
- tanh/sigmoid units whose input weight is tiny relative to the input range (|w| * range << 1)
  are constants: replace with tanh(bias) and fold into the output bias.
- A tanh unit `tanh(w*x + b)` with large |w| is a step at x = -b/w; read the threshold off as
  -b/w before snapping.
- Two steps at a and b combined with same-sign output weights (one rising, one falling) form a
  window/bump; for a binary output this is just `a < x < b`, and a symmetric pair becomes `abs(x) < c`.
- For 1-input binary nets, scan the score on a dense grid to find the exact zero crossings; then
  snap them to nearby simple values (here +-0.5) and check that disagreements stay in the unsure zone.
- If snapped thresholds only pass "snap", keep them anyway when they are clearly the intended
  values; the raw crossings give the "exact" variant if needed.

</details>

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network does
One input x0 in [-2, 2], four tanh hidden units, binary output (class 1 if score > 0).

All four hidden units have tiny input weights (|w| <= 0.019) and moderate biases, so over
[-2, 2] each tanh moves by at most ~0.03. The score is almost a constant:
score(x0) ~= -1.124 - 0.0118 * x0, ranging from -1.101 (x0 = -2) to -1.147 (x0 = 2).
It never comes near the threshold 0, so the classifier is constant: always class 0.

#### What I changed
- Replaced the whole network with `return -1` (a negative constant score = always class 0).
  I used -1 instead of 0 so the score is not sitting exactly on the threshold.
- check.py: PASS (exact), 0 disagreements over 200203 inputs.

#### Heuristics
- Before simplifying units one by one, evaluate the output over the input range (endpoints and a
  coarse grid). If its sign never changes, the binary net is a constant classifier.
- Bound each unit's pre-activation over the input box: |w| * (hi - lo) / 2 tells how much a unit can
  move. When all weights are tiny relative to the domain, each unit is ~a constant
  (tanh(b) or sigmoid(b)), and the output reduces to a constant plus a tiny linear term.
- For a constant binary output, return a constant clearly on one side of the threshold (e.g. -1),
  not the threshold itself, so the decision is robust.
- A net that learned a constant usually means the training signal was dominated by one class in this
  domain (or the true boundary lies outside it); don't invent a boundary the net doesn't have.

</details>

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network computes

Two boolean inputs, four tanh hidden units, one binary output (class 1 if score > 0).
The domain has only 4 points, so I evaluated the net on all of them (scratch/eval.py):

| x0 | x1 | score  | class |
|----|----|--------|-------|
| 0  | 0  | -10.82 | 0     |
| 0  | 1  | +10.50 | 1     |
| 1  | 0  | +10.74 | 1     |
| 1  | 1  | -10.93 | 0     |

That is XOR: class 1 exactly when the inputs differ. All four scores are far from the threshold
(|score| > 10), so the decision is robust.

Reading the hidden units the same way (weights about ±4, biases about ±2 or -5):
- h1_0 ~ "not (x0 and not x1)" (low only at x0=1, x1=0), h1_2 ~ its mirror (low only at x0=0, x1=1);
- h1_1 ~ NOR (high only at 0,0); h1_3 ~ AND (high only at 1,1).
With all output weights negative, the score is high only where NOR and AND are both off and the two
"not-only-one" units sit at their low end, i.e. the two "exactly one" points. Four units for a
two-unit job: the net is redundant (each pair of mirror units would suffice).

#### What I changed

Replaced the whole network with `return x0 != x1` (comparisons give 1/0, and 1 > 0, 0 > 0 is false).
Checked: `PASS (exact)` on the exhaustive 4-point domain, no disagreements.

#### Heuristics

- For all-boolean inputs with few inputs (2^n small), skip the weights: evaluate the net on the
  whole truth table and read the boolean function off it. Then write the simplest boolean
  expression (here `x0 != x1`).
- Check the margin at each truth-table point; if every |score| is far above the threshold, the
  boolean rewrite is safe and no "unsure zone" handling is needed.
- For a binary output, returning a 0/1 comparison works as the raw score when the threshold is 0.
- Saturated tanh units with weights ~±4 and biases ~±2 on 0/1 inputs act as logic gates: the sign
  of the pre-activation at each corner gives the gate (bias ~ +2 with one +4 and one -4 is an
  "implies"-type gate; bias ~ +1.6 with two -4 is NOR; bias ~ -5 with two +3 is AND).
- Mirror-image units (same magnitudes, swapped input signs) and AND/NOR pairs usually signal a
  symmetric function such as XOR/XNOR; expect redundancy beyond the minimal 2 hidden units.

</details>

## xor/seed1: dump vs agent_1

```python
# dumped from a neural network: 2 inputs -> 4 -> 1 outputs
# inputs: p: bool [0, 1], q: bool [0, 1]
# output: binary: class 1 if the returned value > 0, else class 0
def net(p, q):
    h1_0 = tanh(-3.203954 * p - 7.4539776 * q + 0.9199008)  # layer 1 neuron 0
    h1_1 = tanh(2.323399 * p + 6.1640797 * q - 0.041437607)  # layer 1 neuron 1
    h1_2 = tanh(-2.4126883 * p + 6.2517257 * q + 2.2469802)  # layer 1 neuron 2
    h1_3 = tanh(-2.9116824 * p + 7.2077255 * q + 2.0997975)  # layer 1 neuron 3
    return -4.7785964 * h1_0 + 3.334998 * h1_1 - 3.0824802 * h1_2 - 4.7867527 * h1_3 - 0.2443471
```

```python
def net(p, q):
    # Truth table of the net (raw score):
    #   p=0,q=0 -> -11.5   p=1,q=0 -> +11.4
    #   p=0,q=1 -> ~-4e-5  p=1,q=1 -> ~+7e-5   (q=1 rows sit on the boundary)
    # The decision (score > 0) is exactly p; q is irrelevant to the decision.
    return p
```

AST tool (no LLM, `ast4`):

```python
# produced by nndc ast (route: truth-table)
def net(p, q):
    return p
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What I did
- 2 boolean inputs, binary output, so the input domain is only 4 points. I evaluated the dump on all
  four points instead of simplifying the four tanh units algebraically.
- Raw scores: (0,0) -11.51, (1,0) +11.42, (0,1) -3.8e-5, (1,1) +7.3e-5.
- With x1=0 the net is confident and follows x0. With x1=1 the score is about 1e-4, essentially
  zero and inside the checker's unsure zone, but its sign still follows x0.
- So the decision is exactly `x0`, and x1 does not affect it. `agent.py` is `return x0`.
  The check gives PASS (exact), with 0 disagreements, confident or unsure.
- Alternative: `x0 and not x1` (inhibition) also passes, with one unsure-zone disagreement at (1,1).
  The x1=1 scores are about 0, so the net seems to sit on the fence there; the target task may be
  either "x0" or "x0 and not x1". I kept `x0` because it matches every sign the net produces.

#### Heuristics
- For all-boolean (or small finite) input domains, enumerate the truth table from the dump first.
  The program is then a boolean formula of the inputs, and the hidden units do not need decoding.
- For binary output, check the raw score's magnitude on each row, not only its sign. Rows with |score|
  far below the confident rows are "fence" rows: the net is undecided there. Pick the formula that
  matches every sign, the exact one, and note the other fence-row readings as alternatives.
- With a 0/1 input and threshold 0, `return x0` is a valid binary program: 0 is not > 0. No offset
  like `x0 - 0.5` is needed.
- An input whose flip changes only the score's magnitude, never its sign, can be dropped from the
  decision program, even when its weights are large (here |w| ~ 6-7 on x1 in every unit).

</details>

## xor/seed2: dump vs agent_1

```python
# dumped from a neural network: 2 inputs -> 4 -> 1 outputs
# inputs: p: bool [0, 1], q: bool [0, 1]
# output: binary: class 1 if the returned value > 0, else class 0
def net(p, q):
    h1_0 = tanh(4.931534 * p - 3.4728825 * q + 1.0687357)  # layer 1 neuron 0
    h1_1 = tanh(2.7796848 * p - 3.8430114 * q - 1.2766601)  # layer 1 neuron 1
    h1_2 = tanh(3.1403697 * p + 3.5585437 * q - 0.60573095)  # layer 1 neuron 2
    h1_3 = tanh(3.5885487 * p - 4.990999 * q - 1.530669)  # layer 1 neuron 3
    return -9.449446 * h1_0 + 3.8666673 * h1_1 + 4.302479 * h1_2 + 6.5500083 * h1_3 + 6.060332
```

```python
def net(p, q):
    # Truth table of the network: class 1 exactly when the two flags differ (XOR).
    differ = p != q
    return differ
```

AST tool (no LLM, `ast4`):

```python
# produced by nndc ast (route: truth-table)
def net(p, q):
    return p != q
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Agent notes

#### What the network is

Two boolean inputs, 4 tanh hidden units, binary output (class 1 if score > 0).
Because the domain has only 4 points, I evaluated the dump on all of them (scratch/tt.py):

| x0 | x1 | score  | class |
|----|----|--------|-------|
| 0  | 0  | -13.00 | 0     |
| 0  | 1  |  +9.22 | 1     |
| 1  | 0  | +10.70 | 1     |
| 1  | 1  |  -9.28 | 0     |

This is XOR. Every margin is about 9 or more, so the decision is not fragile anywhere.

#### What I changed

- Replaced the 4 tanh units and the output layer with `x0 != x1` (the comparison gives 1/0;
  1 > 0 gives class 1 and 0 > 0 gives class 0).
- All weights, biases and hidden units were dropped. On a boolean domain the per-unit
  coefficients do not matter, only the sign of the score at each corner.

Result: `PASS (exact)` on the exhaustive check (4 inputs).

#### Heuristics

- When all inputs are boolean or small integers and the domain is tiny (here 2^2 points),
  enumerate the dump's output on every point before reading the weights. The truth table is the
  program; the hidden layer is only one way of producing it.
- For binary outputs, look at the sign pattern of the score on the corners and match it to a
  named boolean function (AND, OR, XOR, majority, threshold count). XOR on bools is `a != b`.
- Check the margins: if every corner's score is far from the threshold, a pure boolean
  expression will pass exactly, with no need to keep scale factors.
- A binary program can return a comparison directly (1/0), as long as the threshold is 0 and
  "true" maps to the positive class.
- For tanh/sigmoid nets with large input weights (|w| about 3 to 5 on 0/1 inputs), each unit acts
  close to a step function; but reading unit-by-unit is unnecessary when the input space can be
  enumerated.

</details>
